# 4. Sūrat al-Nisāʾ

**176 verses.** Medinan. It takes its name from the density of rulings concerning women within it: **ٱلنِّسَآء** (*al-nisāʾ*) — "women".

This is the second-longest sūra in the Qurʾān and the most extensive legislative text after al-Baqara. It contains the shares of inheritance, the prohibitions of marriage, the settlement of disputes within the family, the protection of the orphan's property, the degrees of homicide, the performance of prayer in a state of war, migration, testimony and the order of the state. Nearly every question a community meets while building its own internal law appears here at least once.

But to read the sūra as a list of topics is to fail to understand it. **The reading followed in this commentary is the following, and I record it as my own reading:** Sūrat al-Nisāʾ is built from beginning to end on **the right of the one who has no protection**.

The people who enter the sūra's field of concern are these:

- **Orphans** — children whose property is held by another and who have no voice (2-10, 127).
- **Women** — the party whose bridal gift is taken from her, who is *counted* as inheritance instead of inheriting, and who is pressed when she is not wanted (4, 19-21, 32, 34-35, 127-130).
- **Those with a share in an estate** — those left without their share being reckoned (7-12, 176).
- **Slaves and those under one's hand** — placed last in the list of verse 36, but placed in the list (25, 36, 92).
- **Those left behind in war** — *al-mustaḍʿafīna mina'l-rijāli wa'l-nisāʾi wa'l-wildān* (75, 98, 127).
- **The stranded traveller, the neighbour, the companion** — those who fall outside the tie of kinship (36).

**And this thread is tied in the sūra's very first verse.** The first verse names humankind as created from a single soul, and then ends thus:

> **وَٱتَّقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ٱلَّذِى تَسَآءَلُونَ بِهِۦ وَٱلْأَرْحَامَ** — *"Be mindful of God, in whose name you make requests of one another; and of the tie of kinship."* (4/1)

**The tie of kinship is set beside mindfulness of God.** The rest of the sūra tells where that tie breaks and how it is to be held: in the division of an estate, in the orphan's property, at the moment of divorce, in the sending of arbitrators, in the sharing out after a battle.

I shall follow this tie through every block.

## The name of the sūra — al-Nisāʾ

**نِسَاء** — the word has no singular; it is counted as the plural, from a different root, of *imraʾa* (woman). The lexicographers connect the word with the roots **ن-س-و / ن-س-ي** but **do not agree on any settled derivation**; nothing definite is said in this commentary about the origin of the word.

**It must not be forgotten that the name was given later.** Sūra names are not a claim of the text itself but a choice of the tradition. But the ground for choosing this name is in the text: the word occurs dozens of times in the sūra, it is the subject of a ruling in the third verse, it is one half of a balance of word order in the thirty-second, and it is the subject of a question in the hundred and twenty-seventh.

One other sūra in the Qurʾān is popularly called "the lesser al-Nisāʾ" (Sūrat al-Ṭalāq); this naming too comes from the tradition, not from the text itself.

**One record is necessary:** although the name of the sūra is "women", the sūra is not a "women's chapter". **The verses concerning women are placed *inside* the discussion of the orphan's property and of inheritance** — and this placement will be shown in detail below in the treatment of 4/2-3. What the sūra is concerned with is not a sex but **the person whose right is in someone else's hands**.

## Its Medinan character and the historical frame

There is no disagreement that the sūra came down in the Medinan period, and the text itself supports this:

- There are **enforceable rulings** such as the shares of inheritance, the prohibitions of marriage and the degrees of penalty (11-12, 23, 92, 176).
- An arrangement for **sending arbitrators** is provided for (35): there is a mechanism that will decide and a community that will abide by it.
- There is a **state of war** and an order for prayer within war (101-104).
- **Migration** is named as something that has happened and is happening (89, 97-100).
- A group that presents itself as Muslim while looking elsewhere is described in detail (60-63, 88-91, 138-145): this is meaningful only within a settled and strong community.
- Reference is made to a judicial episode (105-113) and to a dispute after a battle (94).

**It cannot be said that the whole of the sūra came down at one time.** It is **transmitted** in the sources that the verses of inheritance belong to the aftermath of a battle and that some sections belong to later years; there is disagreement in the details, and **datings I cannot give with certainty are not given in this commentary.** What can be said is this: the sūra is the text of the period in which an established community was **writing its own internal law**.

### A note on method regarding the narratives of the occasions of revelation

For many verses of this sūra the sources transmit detailed accounts of events: the widows and orphans left after a battle, a woman whose property was taken from her, an accusation of theft, a tribal dispute.

**In this commentary those accounts are related in the language of "it is transmitted" and without naming persons.** The reason is the rule of accuracy in the method (`USLUP.md`): to attach an event to particular people requires being sure of one's source. **I write down no chain of attribution I am not sure of.** And the frame needed to understand a verse is most often already in the verse's own wording.

## The structure of the sūra

The sūra is a long text in which the subjects run into one another; the boundaries of the blocks are not firm lines but **centres of gravity**. The table below shows this and is **my own reading**; its ground is the shifts of address, the recurring words and the transitions of subject.

| Block | Verses | Subject | The sign that opens the block | Key words |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **I** | **1** | The creation of humankind from a single origin; mindfulness of God and kinship | *Yā ayyuha'l-nāsu'ttaqū rabbakum* | *nafsin wāḥida*, *bassa*, *al-arḥām*, *raqīb* |
| **II** | **2-10** | The orphan's property; marriage; the bridal gift; the holding of property in trust | *Wa ātu'l-yatāmā amwālahum* | *lā tatabaddalū*, *taʿdilū*, *niḥla*, *sufahāʾ*, *rushd* |
| **III** | **11-14** | The shares of inheritance and the limits set by God | *Yūṣīkumu'llāhu fī awlādikum* | *ḥaẓẓ*, *waṣiyya*, *dayn*, *kalāla*, *ḥudūdu'llāh* |
| **IV** | **15-22** | Indecency, repentance; that women cannot be counted as inheritance; conduct in marriage | *Wa'llātī yaʾtīna'l-fāḥisha* | *tawba*, *karhan*, *ʿāshirūhunna bi'l-maʿrūf*, *afḍā* |
| **V** | **23-28** | The prohibitions of marriage; the ground of the measure; the lightening | *Ḥurrimat ʿalaykum ummahātukum* | *muḥṣanāt*, *ujūr*, *yurīdu'llāhu li-yubayyina*, *ḍuʿifa* |
| **VI** | **29-35** | Property, life, coveting; the earnings of the man and of the woman; conflict within the family | *Lā taʾkulū amwālakum baynakum bi'l-bāṭil* | *tarāḍin*, *naṣīb*, *iktisāb*, *qawwāmūn*, *nushūz*, *ḥakam*, *iṣlāḥ* |
| **VII** | **36-42** | A list of rights in ten items; miserliness, ostentation; the day of testimony | *Wa'ʿbudu'llāha wa lā tushrikū bihī shayʾā* | *iḥsān*, *al-jār*, *al-ṣāḥib bi'l-janb*, *bukhl*, *riʾāʾa'l-nās* |
| **VIII** | **43-57** | Prayer and purity; a group among the People of the Book; trust and obedience | *Lā taqrabu'ṣ-ṣalāta wa antum sukārā* | *tayammum*, *yuḥarrifūna'l-kalim*, *naṣīban mina'l-kitāb* |
| **IX** | **58-70** | Trusts, justice, obedience, adjudication; the ranks of those who obey | *Inna'llāha yaʾmurukum an tuʾaddu'l-amānāt* | *amānāt*, *ʿadl*, *ulu'l-amr*, *tanāzuʿ*, *ṭāghūt*, *ṣiddīqīn* |
| **X** | **71-104** | War, the oppressed, death, good and ill fortune, news, reflection, killing, migration, prayer | *Khudhū ḥidhrakum* | *mustaḍʿafīn*, *burūj mushayyada*, *tadabbur*, *khaṭaʾan*, *muhājir* |
| **XI** | **105-126** | A judicial episode; defence; the associating of partners with God; the promise of Satan; the return for one's deeds | *Innā anzalnā ilayka'l-kitāba bi'l-ḥaqq* | *khawwān*, *yastakhfūna*, *amāniyy*, *ḥasana*, *ḥanīf* |
| **XII** | **127-134** | What is asked concerning women; the *nushūz* of the husband; settlement; the limit of justice | *Wa yastaftūnaka fi'l-nisāʾ* | *nushūz*, *ṣulḥ*, *shuḥḥ*, *lan tastaṭīʿū*, *iṣlāḥ* |
| **XIII** | **135-152** | Standing by justice even against oneself; those caught between two sides | *Kūnū qawwāmīna bi'l-qisṭ* | *qisṭ*, *shuhadāʾa li'llāh*, *mudhabdhabīn*, *al-darki'l-asfal* |
| **XIV** | **153-162** | The demands and the history of a group among the People of the Book | *Yasʾaluka ahlu'l-kitāb* | *naqḍ al-mīthāq*, *buhtān*, *shubbiha lahum*, *al-rāsikhūn* |
| **XV** | **163-175** | The chain of revelation; proof and light; not going beyond the bound | *Innā awḥaynā ilayka* | *rusul*, *burhān*, *nūr mubīn*, *ghuluww* |
| **XVI** | **176** | *Kalāla*: the last ruling of the sūra | *Yastaftūnaka* | *kalāla*, *yubayyinu'llāhu lakum* |

**The most striking side of the structure is that the sūra's closing section opens with a *fatwā* and closes with a *fatwā*.** The hundred and twenty-seventh verse begins *wa yastaftūnaka fi'l-nisāʾ* (they ask you for a ruling concerning women) and names **the orphan girls**; the hundred and seventy-sixth begins *yastaftūnaka* and gives the ruling on **kalāla**. **Both questions concern inheritance and the sharing of rights; both tie back to the first block of the sūra.**

I record this as **my own reading**; the places of the verses and the words stand in the text.

## The backbone of the sūra: the right of the one who has no protection

This section attempts to read the whole of the sūra from a single frame. **This is a reading, not a transmitted report; its ground is the verses in the table below themselves.**

### One. Who is named

| Who | Where | What is regulated |
|---|---|---|
| **The orphan** | 2, 3, 6, 8, 10, 36, 127 | That his property be handed over, that he be tested, that it not be mixed with another's |
| **The woman** | 3, 4, 7, 11-12, 19-21, 24-25, 32, 34-35, 127-130, 176 | Bridal gift, inheritance, treatment, dispute, separation |
| **The kinsman** | 1, 7, 8, 33, 36, 135 | The keeping of the tie; but no favouring of him in testimony |
| **The poor** | 8, 36, 135 | The share of one present at a division; the address to which spending goes |
| **The slave / the one under one's hand** | 25, 36, 92 | Marriage, kindness, setting free |
| **The neighbour and the companion at one's side** | 36 | Ties outside kinship |
| **The mustaḍʿafūn (those made weak)** | 75, 97-98, 127 | The ground for war; the limit of an excuse |
| **The traveller (*ibnu'l-sabīl*)** | 36 | The right of the one who is not known |

**What all eight rows have in common is this: the person whose right is in someone else's hands.**

### Two. The sentence form the sūra builds most often

In laying down a ruling the sūra uses one pattern again and again: **it gives the command, then closes with a verse-ending that recalls who it is that gives the command.**

| Verse | Command | Verse-ending |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Mindfulness of God and kinship | *Inna'llāha kāna ʿalaykum raqībā* |
| 2 | Do not consume the property of orphans | *Innahū kāna ḥūban kabīrā* |
| 6 | Call witnesses when you hand over the orphan's property | *Wa kafā bi'llāhi ḥasībā* |
| 10 | Those who consume the property of orphans wrongfully | *Wa sayaṣlawna saʿīrā* |
| 33 | Give each his share | *Inna'llāha kāna ʿalā kulli shayʾin shahīdā* |
| 58 | Deliver trusts to those entitled to them, judge with justice | *Inna'llāha kāna samīʿan baṣīrā* |
| 86 | Return a greeting with a better one | *Inna'llāha kāna ʿalā kulli shayʾin ḥasībā* |
| 135 | Uphold justice even against yourselves | *Fa-inna'llāha kāna bimā taʿmalūna khabīrā* |

**Most of these verse-endings carry a verb of *seeing*, *hearing*, *reckoning* or *watching*:** *raqīb*, *ḥasīb*, *shahīd*, *samīʿ*, *baṣīr*, *khabīr*.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the list above:** the sūra sets a **witness** beside the person whose right is unprotected. The orphan, the woman, the slave, the traveller has no advocate; each time, the verse names in their place one who watches.

### Three. The word at the two ends of the sūra

**The sūra opens with the word *al-arḥām* (the ties of kinship) (1) and closes with the discussion of *kalāla* — the estate of a person who leaves neither child nor father (176).**

This is a datum that can be verified from the text. And it yields the following observation, which **I record as my own reading:** the sūra begins from the **widest** form of the tie (all humankind from a single soul) and ends at its **most attenuated** form (a person who leaves behind neither child nor father). **At both ends the question is the same: when the tie breaks, how is the right to be protected?**

## A note on method: what is not done in this section

Sūrat al-Nisāʾ is the sūra in which this commentary's rules of style must be observed most closely. So I set down at the outset what is not done.

**One. No legal ruling is issued.** From this sūra arose the widest discussions of classical jurisprudence: the reckoning of inheritance, marriage, divorce, penalties, the law of war. **In this section the disagreements within those discussions are related in the language of "it is transmitted" and in the form of tables; none of them is preferred and none of them is binding.** To decide how a ruling is to be applied in practice is not the business of this text; that business belongs to the literature of jurisprudence, which has the competence for it.

**Two. The reckoning of inheritance is not entered into.** In the eleventh, twelfth and hundred and seventy-sixth verses **the shares given by the verse's own wording are set out in a table.** What is to be done when the shares add up to more or to less than the whole (*ʿawliyya*, *raddiyya*), which heir is excluded in which case, and the differences among the schools of law **are not the subject of this text.**

**Three. No side is taken on the thirty-fourth verse.** The readings of this verse in the classical commentaries are given **as a table of the disagreement, with their grounds.** **No preference is imposed, no legal ruling is issued, and no current debate or politics is entered into.** And the verse is treated **together** with the thirty-fifth, from which it cannot be separated.

**Four. No demeaning language about women is used.** This is a record that holds from the first verse onward: no ranking of worth is built upon the expressions *min nafsin wāḥida* and *wa khalaqa minhā zawjahā*.

**Five. Current politics is not entered into.** This record holds especially in the treatment of the fifty-ninth verse (*ulu'l-amr*) and the seventy-fifth (*the mustaḍʿafūn*). Today's debates about whom the verse fits are not the subject of this text.

**Six. No sweeping ruling is made about any group.** The verses between the hundred and fifty-third and the hundred and seventy-third touch on the history of a community and on a claim. **What is at issue are attributes and claims**; whoever bears the attribute is included in it. No general ruling is made about any religious, ethnic or national group.

**Seven. The reader is not scolded.** The subjects of this sūra — the quarrel over an inheritance, discord within the family, duplicity — are the subjects that have produced the most scolding in the language of preaching. This section does not do that.

**Eight. No fabricated report is used.** No statement I am not sure of is ascribed to an exegete, to a hadith or to a source.

## The basmala

The word-by-word analysis of the basmala was made in the section on al-Fātiḥa; I do not repeat it here. The numbering of the verses has been counted without the basmala.

One point particular to this sūra can be recorded: **the first verse of a text that opens with the names al-Raḥmān and al-Raḥīm names the tie of kinship (*al-arḥām*).** The words *Raḥmān*, *Raḥīm* and *raḥim* (womb, kinship) are from the same root: **ر-ح-م.** The analyses of this root in this commentary were made in `001-fatiha.md` and `030-rum.md`; I rely on those treatments.

**I record this as an observation about a word:** the two divine names at the head of the sūra and the closing command of the first verse come from the same root. This is not a claim resting on numbers or letters; the root of the words stands in the dictionary.

---

## 4/1 — يا أيها الناس اتقوا ربكم الذي خلقكم من نفس واحدة وخلق منها زوجها وبث منهما رجالا كثيرا ونساء واتقوا الله الذي تساءلون به والأرحام إن الله كان عليكم رقيبا

*Yā ayyuha'l-nāsu'ttaqū rabbakumu'lladhī khalaqakum min nafsin wāḥidatin wa khalaqa minhā zawjahā wa bassa minhumā rijālan kathīran wa nisāʾā, wa'ttaqu'llāha'lladhī tasāʾalūna bihī wa'l-arḥām, inna'llāha kāna ʿalaykum raqībā*

"O mankind! Be mindful of your Lord, who created you from a single soul, and created from it its mate, and spread from the two of them many men and women. Be mindful of God, in whose name you make requests of one another, and of the ties of kinship. Surely God is a watcher over you."

### First, a note on method

**Two expressions in this verse — *min nafsin wāḥida* and *wa khalaqa minhā zawjahā* — have been much debated in the history of exegesis, and that debate has from time to time been made material for readings that pass judgement on the worth of women.**

**What will be done in this section is this:** the classical explanations will be given **as a table of the disagreement**, with their grounds; **none of them will be preferred**; and **no ranking of worth between the sexes will be built upon any reading.** Whatever the verse's own wording says, with that we shall be content.

### يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ — the breadth of the address

**The sūra is Medinan, yet its opening address is not *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* but *yā ayyuha'l-nās*: "O mankind".**

This is a choice worth recording. Most Medinan sūras address those who believe; and the party addressed by a text that lays down rulings is naturally the community that will apply them. **Al-Nisāʾ, though it is the most densely legislative sūra of all, opens by addressing the whole of humankind.**

**And the address does not stand alone in the sūra:** the call *yā ayyuha'l-nās* comes twice more towards the end of the sūra (170, 174), and in the hundred and thirty-third verse it occurs as *ayyuha'l-nās*, without the vocative particle.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the position of these four places:** the sūra opens by addressing humankind and approaches its close by addressing humankind; **in between, the internal law of one community is being written.** That is, the rulings are set *inside* the frame of humankind.

### ٱتَّقُوا۟ رَبَّكُمُ — "Be mindful of your Lord"

The analysis of the root **ت-ق-ي / و-ق-ي** was made at `002-bakara.md` 2/2: the concrete sense of the root is **to guard something from what would harm it, to put a shield in between.** I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it here.

**What must be recorded is which name is used: *rabbakum*.** It does not say "be mindful of God" — it says **"of your Lord"**. The root **ر-ب-ب**: the one who nurtures, who brings up, who owns. And immediately afterwards the creating is recounted.

The order of the sentence is therefore this: **be mindful → because He is the one who brought you up → because He is the one who brought you into being from a single origin.** The ground of the command comes after the command.

### مِن نَّفْسٍ وَٰحِدَةٍ — "from a single soul"

**نَفْس — root ن-ف-س.** The concrete sense of the root is **to breathe, breath.** *Nafas* — breath; *nafīs* — precious (reckoned equal to life); *tanāfus* — to vie with one another; *nifās* — the state that follows childbirth. The root was treated from various sides in `024-nur.md`, `081-tekvir.md` and `083-mutaffifin.md`.

**The word's range of use in the Qurʾān is wide:** life, person, the human being himself, selfhood. Here it means "one person, one being".

### And first a grammatical datum — this must be known before the debate

**In Arabic the word نَفْس is feminine.**

This is a fact of grammar, not an interpretation. Its consequence is this:

| Word | Why it takes this form | What it does not mean |
|---|---|---|
| **وَٰحِدَة** (*wāḥida*) | Because *nafs* is feminine, its adjective comes in the feminine too | It does not mean "a female being" |
| **مِنْهَا** (*minhā*) | The pronoun refers back to the word *nafs*, which is feminine | That a pronoun is feminine says nothing about the sex of what it points to |
| **زَوْجَهَا** (*zawjahā*) | The word *zawj* is used in Arabic for **both a male and a female spouse** | By itself it does not state the sex of the spouse |

**These three items show the ground on which the debate is being conducted.** The wording of the verse does not say "the male was created first, then the female"; what says this, or does not say it, is the **interpretation** of what the word *nafs* answers to.

**The sense of the root ز-و-ج must also be recorded:** *zawj* is not "spouse" on its own but **each single member of a pair**. One shoe of a pair is a *zawj* too. The word therefore states **the pairing**; it does not subordinate either party to the other. The Qurʾān uses the word for plants and for the whole of creation as well (*wa min kulli shayʾin khalaqnā zawjayn* — al-Dhāriyāt 51/49; treated in `051-zariyat.md`).

### The table of the disagreement — *min nafsin wāḥida* and *wa khalaqa minhā zawjahā*

**The following are the principal readings transmitted in the classical commentaries concerning these two expressions. The table is neutral; no preference is imposed and none of them is binding.**

| Reading | What *nafsin wāḥida* is | What *minhā* means | Its ground | The side seen as weak |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A. The first human being** | The first human being to be created | The mate was created from that first human being / from its kind | Its connection with the verses of the Qurʾān that recount the first human being; this is the reading most widely transmitted in the classical commentaries | Whether *minhā* means "from a part of it" or "from its kind" is not clear in the verse |
| **B. The kind / the species** | The human species, a single origin of creation | *Min jinsihā* — "from its own kind" | The Qurʾān says *min anfusikum azwājan* (al-Rūm 30/21; al-Naḥl 16/72): it states that the mate is "from you / from your own kind". The same preposition there carries the sense of kind | It generalises the verse's qualification *wāḥida* (single) |
| **C. A single origin (unity of descent)** | The common root of all human beings | The mate too is from the same root | The continuation of the verse: *wa bassa minhumā* — increase is set going **from the two of them** | It does not conflict with A and B; it may not count as a separate view |

**All three readings are found in the classical literature, and I make no firm choice among them.** The reason is the rule on disagreement in the method (`USLUP.md`): the disagreement is not concealed, no preference is imposed.

**But there are three things that do not enter the table, that can be read directly from the wording of the verse, and that are not in dispute:**

**One.** The verse says that both parties were created **by the same verb**: *khalaqa…* and *khalaqa…* — **the verb is repeated, it does not change.**

**Two.** Increase is attached **to the two of them together**: *wa bassa **minhumā*** — "from the two of them". Not the singular, but the dual.

**Three.** The outcome is given in two words: *rijālan kathīran **wa nisāʾan*** — men and women. **The verse counts them both.**

**No ranking of superiority follows from these three data.** And none is drawn in this commentary.

### وَبَثَّ — the root ب-ث-ث

**Root ب-ث-ث: to spread, to disperse, to scatter.** The root was treated at `045-casiye.md` 45/4 (*wa mā yabuththu min dābba*); the record made there was the following, and it sits directly here:

> *"**بَثّ** — root **ب-ث-ث**: to spread, to disperse, to scatter. The lexicographers record for the root the picture of 'strewing something about'… the verse does not classify living creatures, it makes **their being spread abroad** the proof. What is named is not diversity but **distribution**."*

**And the same observation holds here.** The verse is not classifying the human species; it is recounting its **dispersal**. The word draws the picture of a scattering outward from a centre.

**And a second use of the root must be recorded:** from the same root comes the word **بَثّ**, in the sense of "grief that cannot be held in, that overflows" (Yūsuf 12/86: *innamā ashkū baththī wa ḥuznī ila'llāh*). The root thus names both the multitude that spreads outward and the grief that will not fit inside and spills over. **I record this as an observation about the root; I build no interpretation of the verse upon it.**

### رِجَالًا كَثِيرًا وَنِسَآءً — a detail of word order

**The adjective *kathīran* (many) comes after the word *rijāl*; no further adjective is repeated after the word *nisāʾ*.**

This is an ordinary situation in Arabic grammar: a single adjective can refer back to two nouns joined by a conjunction. **In the classical commentaries it is explained thus:** *kathīran* qualifies both nouns, and there is no need to repeat it.

**I give this as a grammatical record and draw from it no sense of superiority or of deficiency.** The sentence of the verse states that the number is great; it does not state which of the two is the greater.

### وَٱتَّقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ٱلَّذِى تَسَآءَلُونَ بِهِۦ — the second command to be mindful

**The verse gives the command to mindfulness twice. In the first the name is *rabbakum*, in the second *Allāh*.**

**تَسَآءَلُونَ — root س-أ-ل, Form VI (*tafāʿul*): reciprocity.** That is, "you ask of one another, you make requests of one another".

**And *bihī* — "by Him, in His name".** The expression records a habit of language: when people ask something of one another they **put the name of God in between** — "for God's sake", "in God's name". This is a formula that lives in Arabic and in English alike.

**What the verse does is worth recording:** it takes the most frequently used, most worn-down religious expression and makes it **the object of mindfulness**. What is asked is wariness before the Being whose name circulates on the tongue.

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the verb *tasāʾalūna bihī* itself.

### وَٱلْأَرْحَامَ — the key word of the sūra

**رَحِم — root ر-ح-م.** The concrete sense of the word is **the womb**. And the words *raḥma*, *Raḥmān* and *Raḥīm* are from the same root.

**The lexicographers dwell on this feature of the root:** the name of the feeling of mercy comes from the same root as the name of the organ that gives birth. **I record this as an observation about the root.**

*Al-arḥām* here is plural and means, figuratively, **the ties of kinship**: the bond that arises from having come from the same womb.

### A variant reading and its meaning

**There is a difference worth recording in the reading of this word, and the difference affects the meaning. I give no imam's name, because I cannot give it with certainty.**

| Reading | Its case | Its meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **ٱلْأَرْحَامَ** (*al-arḥāma* — accusative) | Coordinated with the word *Allāha* | "Be mindful of God **and be mindful of the ties of kinship too**" |
| **ٱلْأَرْحَامِ** (*al-arḥāmi* — genitive) | Coordinated with the pronoun in *bihī* | "Be mindful of God, in whose name **and in the name of the tie of kinship** you make requests of one another" |

**Both readings are recorded in the classical literature of variant readings. I make no choice between them.**

**And the two readings arrive at the same place by different routes:** in the first, the tie of kinship enters the object of mindfulness as **something that must be protected**; in the second it is named as **a ground people invoke when they ask something of one another**. **In both cases the weight of the tie is recorded.**

### The placing of kinship beside mindfulness of God

**This is the most striking move of the verse: *ittaqu'llāha … wa'l-arḥām*.**

On the first reading, two things to be mindful of come side by side: **God and the tie of kinship.** This is not a common word order in the Qurʾān.

**And this is the place that explains the whole of the sūra. I record it as my own reading; its ground is the rest of the sūra:**

- **The verses of inheritance** (11-12, 176) turn the tie of kinship into **a share**.
- **The verses on the orphan's property** (2-10) turn the tie of kinship into **a trust**.
- **The verse on conflict within the family** (35) asks for the arbitrators to be drawn **from the families of both parties**: *ḥakaman min ahlihī wa ḥakaman min ahlihā*.
- **The thirty-sixth verse** names the kinsman in its list of kindness **immediately after the parents**.
- **The hundred and thirty-fifth verse**, by contrast, sets a limit in the opposite direction: justice **may not be bent in a relative's favour** (*awi'l-wālidayni wa'l-aqrabīn*).

**So the sūra both raises the tie of kinship and limits it.** The tie is a source of right; but it is not a ground for favour. **Read together, the first verse and the hundred and thirty-fifth make this balance visible.**

### إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ كَانَ عَلَيْكُمْ رَقِيبًا — the verse-ending

**رَقِيب — root ر-ق-ب.** The concrete sense of the root is striking: **رَقَبَة (*raqaba*) — the neck.** And the verb *raqaba*: **to watch by craning the neck, to look out from a high place.**

*Marqab* — a lookout post. *Irtiqāb* — to wait and watch. And in the Qurʾān **فَكُّ رَقَبَةٍ** (the loosing of a neck — the freeing of a slave; al-Balad 90/13, treated in `090-beled.md`) is from the same root.

**So the picture carried by the word *raqīb* is this: one who cranes his neck and looks, who does not take his eyes off.**

**And the placing of the word here must be recorded.** The sūra is about to begin regulating people whose right is in someone else's hands. **The last word of the first verse sets a watcher beside those people.**

**And the verb comes with *kāna*: *kāna ʿalaykum raqībā*.** In Arabic the pattern *kāna* + noun states not a passing but a **continuing** state. This pattern recurs very often in the verse-endings of this sūra — *kāna ʿalaykum raqībā*, *kāna ḥūban kabīrā*, *kāna samīʿan baṣīrā*, *kāna ʿalīman ḥakīmā*. **This is the sound-weave of the sūra, and each instance will be named below in its place.**

### What this says to the present

**One.** The verse opens a sūra of law with **a family tree**. Before all the quarrels over division, the lawsuits over inheritance and the disputes over rights, it sets down the information that the parties come from the same root. **The section of rulings begins by announcing that the parties are not strangers to one another.**

**Two.** The record *tasāʾalūna bihī* touches something still alive today: the wearing-down of a name through constant use. The verse takes that name back and makes it the object of mindfulness.

**Three.** The placing of *al-arḥām* beside mindfulness of God lifts kinship out of being a matter of feeling and carries it into the field of **responsibility**. A tie exists not to the degree that it is felt but to the degree that it is kept.

**And a limit:** it does not follow from this that "a relative is to be favoured in every case". The same sūra says exactly the opposite in the hundred and thirty-fifth verse. **To keep a tie and to show favour through a tie are two different things in this sūra.**

---

## 4/2 — وآتوا اليتامى أموالهم ولا تتبدلوا الخبيث بالطيب ولا تأكلوا أموالهم إلى أموالكم إنه كان حوبا كبيرا

*Wa ātu'l-yatāmā amwālahum wa lā tatabaddalu'l-khabītha bi'l-ṭayyib, wa lā taʾkulū amwālahum ilā amwālikum, innahū kāna ḥūban kabīrā*

"Give the orphans their property. Do not exchange what is good for what is foul. Do not consume their property by adding it to your own. This is a great sin."

### Why the second verse of the sūra is here

**The first verse recounted humankind as coming from a single root and set the tie of kinship beside mindfulness of God. The second verse begins from the place where that tie breaks most easily: the property of a child who has lost his father.**

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the order of the two verses. Whether a tie really exists shows itself where the other party cannot defend himself. **The sūra enters at exactly that point.**

### يَتِيم — the root ي-ت-م

**The root's original sense: to be left alone, to be separated from one's like.**

A usage the lexicographers record is worth noting: **دُرَّة يَتِيمَة** (*durra yatīma*) — **"an orphan pearl"**, that is, a pearl with no match, standing alone. When the word is used of a human being it means **a small child who has lost his father**.

The root was treated in `093-duha.md` (93/6 — *a-lam yajidka yatīman fa-āwā*); I rely on that treatment.

**And the picture the word carries explains the subject of the verse: the orphan is the person with no one standing behind him.** His property is in his hands, but he has not the strength to protect it.

### وَءَاتُوا۟ — "give"

**The verb is an imperative, and its object is stated at the outset: *amwālahum* — "their property".**

**A detail of the word order must be recorded:** the verse does not say "give property to the orphans"; it says **"give the orphans *their own* property"**. The pronoun belongs to them. **What is at issue, then, is not a gift but a restitution.**

### وَلَا تَتَبَدَّلُوا۟ ٱلْخَبِيثَ بِٱلطَّيِّبِ — the prohibition of exchange

**The root ب-د-ل: to put something in the place of another.** Form V (*tafaʿʿul*) here states **a deliberate and gradual operation**.

**In the classical commentaries the following is widely transmitted as the concrete referent of this sentence:** that the guardian would take what was good in the orphan's property and put in its place what was bad in his own — like taking the fat sheep of the flock and leaving the lean one. **I relate this explanation in the language of "it is transmitted".**

**And the choice of the pair *khabīth* / *ṭayyib* must be recorded.** Both words are used in the Qurʾān in a material and in a moral sense alike: *ṭayyib* — clean, pleasant, lawful; *khabīth* — foul, corrupt, forbidden. **The sentence therefore names a transaction of property in the language of morals.**

I record this as an observation about a word: **the exchange looks equal; the naming is not.**

### إِلَىٰٓ أَمْوَٰلِكُمْ — the choice of preposition

**The verse does not say *maʿa* (together with), it says *ilā* (towards, adding to).**

**In the classical commentaries the following sense is drawn from this preposition, and I relate it as it is transmitted:** to consume the orphan's property by **adding** it to one's own, so that it can no longer be told apart. **The matter, then, is not only the consuming but the mixing itself** — what can be called to account so long as it stands apart becomes invisible once it is merged.

**And this record must be read together with the measure given in al-Baqara.** At `002-bakara.md` 2/220 the balance between keeping the orphan's property separate and mixing it was treated, and what was recorded there was: *fa-ikhwānukum* — "they are your brothers", but *wa'llāhu yaʿlamu'l-mufsida mina'l-muṣliḥ* — the one who corrupts is told apart from the one who sets right. **I rely on that treatment.**

**Read together, the two verses give this measure, and I record it as an observation:** to live in common is free; to keep a common account is not.

### حُوبًا كَبِيرًا — the singularity of the word

**حُوب — root ح-و-ب.** The lexicographers explain the word as **sin, guilt, grave offence**. The root's sense of "need, distress" is also recorded.

**The word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.** Other words for sin are used in the sūra (*ithm*, *sayyiʾa*, *dhanb*); **here a word that occurs only once is chosen.**

**I record this as an observation and draw no firm conclusion from it:** it can be said that the choice of word carries an emphasis, but how much of that is meaning and how much the harmony of the verse-ending cannot be determined.

**And the pattern of the verse-ending is again formed with *kāna*:** *innahū kāna ḥūban kabīrā*. The same structure as *kāna ʿalaykum raqībā* in the first verse.

---

## 4/3 — وإن خفتم ألا تقسطوا في اليتامى فانكحوا ما طاب لكم من النساء مثنى وثلاث ورباع فإن خفتم ألا تعدلوا فواحدة أو ما ملكت أيمانكم ذلك أدنى ألا تعولوا

*Wa in khiftum allā tuqsiṭū fi'l-yatāmā fa'nkiḥū mā ṭāba lakum mina'l-nisāʾi mathnā wa thulātha wa rubāʿ, fa-in khiftum allā taʿdilū fa-wāḥidatan aw mā malakat aymānukum, dhālika adnā allā taʿūlū*

"If you fear that you cannot act with fairness towards the orphans, then marry of the women lawful to you two by two, three by three, four by four. If you fear that you cannot deal equitably, then one; or what your right hands possess. That is nearer to your not falling into injustice / into a burden."

### First, a note on method

**This verse is one of the most debated places in the Qurʾān and one of the most often torn from its context.**

**What will be done in this section is this:**
1. That the verse comes **in the context of orphans** will be shown from the text's own wording.
2. The condition *fa-in khiftum allā taʿdilū fa-wāḥida* will be **brought to the fore.**
3. The verse will be read together with **4/129**, and the two verses will be compared **in a table.**
4. The differing readings of the classical commentaries and of the schools of law will be related **in the language of "it is transmitted".**

**And these will not be done: no preference will be imposed, no legal ruling will be issued, no current debate will be entered into, the reader will not be scolded, and no demeaning language about women will be used.**

### One: the verse's conditional clause concerns orphans — this is a datum of the text

**The verse does not begin with a command. It begins with a *conditional* clause:**

> **وَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ أَلَّا تُقْسِطُوا۟ فِى ٱلْيَتَٰمَىٰ** — *"If you fear that you cannot act with fairness towards the orphans…"*

**So the subject of the condition standing before the command (*fa'nkiḥū*) is orphans.** This is not a point open to interpretation; it is the wording of the sentence.

**And the verse's position says the same thing:** the second verse was about the orphan's property, and the sixth verse will again be about the orphan's property. **The third verse stands between the two.**

| Verse | Subject |
|---|---|
| **2** | Give the orphans their property; do not mix it with your own |
| **3** | *If you fear you cannot act justly towards the orphans…* → the ruling on marriage |
| **4** | Give the women their bridal gifts |
| **5** | Do not hand over property to one who cannot manage it |
| **6** | Test the orphans; when you see soundness of judgement in them, give them their property |

**Four of these five verses concern the trusteeship of property directly.** The third verse stands in the middle of this cluster, and that is a countable fact.

### Two: the link between the condition and its answer — a table of the disagreement

**The condition concerns orphans, the answer concerns marriage. What the link between the two is has been one of the most debated questions in the classical commentaries.**

**The table below shows the main explanations transmitted in the classical sources. None of them is preferred and none is binding.**

| Explanation | How the link is made | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **A. The guardian marrying the orphan girl in his care** | The guardian, with an eye on the property or the beauty of the orphan girl in his household, would marry her with a bridal gift below what her like would receive. The verse: if you cannot do this properly, marry other women | The verse's connection with the hundred and twenty-seventh: there the expression *wa targhabūna an tankiḥūhunna* (you desire to marry them) returns to the same matter |
| **B. The fear of injustice set upon two fields at once** | People were wary of injustice in the orphan's property but not wary in the number of wives. The verse: what you fear in the one, fear in the other too | The same verb is repeated in the two clauses: *in khiftum allā tuqsiṭū* / *fa-in khiftum allā taʿdilū* |
| **C. Avoiding the burden of caring for orphans** | To one who shrinks from caring for orphans it is said to keep his responsibility within limits | Reading the closing word *allā taʿūlū* in the sense of "not to take on a crowd" |
| **D. The regulation of a situation after a battle** | It is **transmitted** in the sources that after a battle the number of widows and orphans left behind grew; it is said that the verse regulates this situation | The presence of post-war regulations throughout the sūra |

**The four explanations do not wholly exclude one another.** A and D are readings that support one another; B rests on the repetition in the wording of the sentence.

**I make no choice.** The one certain thing is that the subject of the conditional clause is orphans.

### Three: مَثْنَىٰ وَثُلَٰثَ وَرُبَٰعَ — the pattern itself

**These three words are in a special Arabic pattern called *maʿdūl*, and their meaning is not "two, three, four" but "two by two, three by three, four by four".**

| Word | Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **مَثْنَىٰ** | *Mafʿal* — *maʿdūl* | Two by two |
| **ثُلَٰثَ** | *Fuʿāl* — *maʿdūl* | Three by three |
| **رُبَٰعَ** | *Fuʿāl* — *maʿdūl* | Four by four |

**The pattern carries two pieces of information, and both are grammar, not interpretation:**

**One.** The pattern of repetition/distribution shows that the address is **not to one person but to a community**. It means "each of you two by two, three by three, four by four" — distribution, not addition.

**Two.** The same pattern occurs in one other place in the Qurʾān: **al-Fāṭir 35/1** — of the wings of the angels, *mathnā wa thulātha wa rubāʿ*. That verse was treated in `035-fatir.md`. **The pattern therefore states not the number itself but the distribution.**

**And the particle itself must be recorded: between the words stands *wa* (the conjunction), not *aw* (or).** In classical grammar it is said that this *wa* states an alternative here; **this is a point the grammarians dwell on, and it has not been brought to a firm conclusion.**

### Four: فَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ أَلَّا تَعْدِلُوا۟ فَوَٰحِدَةً — the condition

**The second half of the verse sets a condition upon the first half, and this condition must be brought to the fore:**

> **فَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ أَلَّا تَعْدِلُوا۟ فَوَٰحِدَةً** — *"If you fear that you cannot deal equitably, then one."*

**The structure of the sentence deserves attention:**

| Element | What it says |
|---|---|
| **خِفْتُمْ** (*khiftum*) | The measure is not injustice that has occurred but **the fear of injustice**. The threshold has been drawn forward |
| **أَلَّا تَعْدِلُوا۟** | The verb is from *ʿadl* — to hold the balance, to treat equally |
| **فَوَٰحِدَةً** | The answer is a single word: **one** |
| **ذَٰلِكَ أَدْنَىٰٓ** | And a ground is given: "this is **nearer** to your not…" |

**The verse's own logic is this, and it is read off the wording of the sentence:** the permission has been tied to a condition, the measure of the condition has been lowered to the level of *fear*, and the place to be reached when the condition holds is stated in a single word.

### Five: the difference between عَدْل and قِسْط

**Two different roots are used in the verse, and this must be recorded.**

| Root | Word | The distinction the lexicographers record |
|---|---|---|
| **ق-س-ط** | *Tuqsiṭū* (in the first condition) | To give the share, the portion; fairness in dividing. *Qisṭ* — a share. In Form IV "to be just", in Form I "to swerve from the right" (one of the *aḍdād*, the words that carry contrary senses) |
| **ع-د-ل** | *Taʿdilū* (in the second condition) | Equivalence, holding two sides at the same weight. *ʿAdl* — the equal of a thing |

**And the placing of the two roots is worth recording:** the root that concerns **the share** is used for the orphans, the root that concerns **equivalence** for marriage.

I record this as an **observation about word order**; I draw no ruling from it.

**The root ق-س-ط was treated at `049-hucurat.md` 49/9 and at `055-rahman.md` 55/9; I rely on those treatments.** The root will return once more in this sūra, in the hundred and thirty-fifth verse, this time in the context of **testimony**: *kūnū qawwāmīna bi'l-qisṭ*.

### Six: أَلَّا تَعُولُوا۟ — the disagreement over the word

**تَعُولُوا۟ — root ع-و-ل.** And there is a classical disagreement over the meaning of the word.

| Reading | What *taʿūlū* means | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **To do injustice, to incline to one side** | The root's sense of "to bend, the balance tipping to one side". *ʿĀla'l-mīzān* — the balance tipped |
| **2** | **That the number of dependants one must support increases** | The root's derivative *ʿiyāl* (the family one supports). *ʿĀla'l-rajul* — the man had many mouths to feed |

**Both are found among the lexicographers. I do not impose a preference.**

**And neither reading changes the outcome of the verse:** in the one it says "not to fall into injustice", in the other "not to take on a burden you cannot carry". **In both cases the function of the sentence is limitation.**

### Seven: 4/3 together with 4/129

**The sūra returns to the same subject in the hundred and twenty-ninth verse, and there it says something else:**

> **وَلَن تَسْتَطِيعُوٓا۟ أَن تَعْدِلُوا۟ بَيْنَ ٱلنِّسَآءِ وَلَوْ حَرَصْتُمْ فَلَا تَمِيلُوا۟ كُلَّ ٱلْمَيْلِ فَتَذَرُوهَا كَٱلْمُعَلَّقَةِ**
> *"You will never be able to deal equitably among women, however much you desire it. So do not incline wholly to one side and leave the other as though suspended."* (4/129)

**I set the two verses side by side. The table compares the wording of the two verses; it establishes no ruling.**

| | **4/3** | **4/129** |
|---|---|---|
| **Verb** | *Taʿdilū* — to deal justly | *Taʿdilū* — the same verb |
| **Mood** | Conditional: *in khiftum allā taʿdilū* | Negated future: *lan tastaṭīʿū an taʿdilū* |
| **What it says** | If you **fear** you cannot deal justly | You **will not be able** to realise justice fully |
| **Degree of force** | *Khiftum* — a possibility | *Lan* — the strongest negation of the future in Arabic |
| **Added qualification** | — | *Wa law ḥaraṣtum* — "however much you desire it" |
| **Concluding clause** | *Fa-wāḥida* — one | *Fa-lā tamīlū kulla'l-mayl* — do not incline wholly to one side |
| **Ground** | *Adnā allā taʿūlū* | *Fa-tadharūhā ka'l-muʿallaqa* — do not leave her suspended |

**What follows from the wording of the two verses and is not in dispute is this:** the first verse sets justice as a **condition**; the second states that this condition **cannot be fulfilled completely**.

**On how these two verses are to be reconciled, different tendencies are transmitted in the classical commentaries:**

| Tendency | How it reconciles them |
|---|---|
| **a** | The justice in 4/129 is **the inclination of the heart**, the justice in 4/3 is **conduct and maintenance**. A person can act equally in conduct; he cannot command the inclination in his heart. **This is the explanation most widely transmitted in the classical commentaries** |
| **b** | 4/129 came to show how heavy the condition in 4/3 really is; read together, the two verses narrow the permission in practice |
| **c** | The two verses are on two separate matters and are not directly linked; 4/129 regulates the management of an existing situation |

**I prefer none of these three tendencies, and none of them is binding.** All three have been defended in the classical literature, and the rulings drawn from these verses differ among the schools of law.

**One thing I record separately, because it is a datum of the text:** the continuation of 4/129 runs *wa in tuṣliḥū wa tattaqū fa-inna'llāha kāna Ghafūran Raḥīmā*, and in 4/128 it says *wa'l-ṣulḥu khayr* (settlement is better). **That is, the sūra closes this discussion not with a sanction but with a call to *setting right*.** The same thing will happen in the thirty-fifth verse.

### Eight: the points on which the schools of law divide — transmitted, not preferred

**The table below shows the principal divergences that arose from this verse in classical jurisprudence. None of them is this text's preference, none of them is binding, and within each school there are differences of view as well.**

| Question | The main tendencies transmitted |
|---|---|
| **The limit of number** | The great majority of classical jurisprudence agrees that the number is limited to four. There is a grammatical debate over how the pattern (*mathnā wa thulātha wa rubāʿ*) yields this |
| **The legal character of the condition of justice** | Some jurists count it a **binding condition**; some count it an obligation that is *diyānī* (a matter between the person and God, not enforceable in court). The divergence has to do with whether justice can be measured |
| **The scope of justice** | There is broad agreement on equality in maintenance and in the turn of nights; on **the inclination of the heart** it is said, on the strength of 4/129, that a person is not held responsible |
| **The relation between 4/3 and 4/129** | The three tendencies above (a, b, c) apply here too |
| **The consequence of breaching the condition** | The schools differ over whether it affects the validity of the marriage and over what claims the woman may bring |

**This table is not a fatwā and cannot be read as a guide to practice.** The reason it is placed here is the rule on disagreement in the method (`USLUP.md`): the disagreement is not concealed, no preference is imposed.

**And current debates are not entered into.** How this verse is understood today, how it is regulated in this or that country, and the social debates around it are not the subject of this text.

### Nine: three limiting devices in the verse's own word order

**I record this as an observation about word order, and its ground is the wording of the verse. In giving the permission, the verse narrows it three times:**

| Order | Clause | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *Wa in khiftum allā tuqsiṭū fi'l-yatāmā* | It gives the permission as **the answer to a condition** — not as an absolute command |
| **2** | *Fa-in khiftum allā taʿdilū fa-wāḥida* | It sets a second condition and brings the answer down to **one** |
| **3** | *Dhālika adnā allā taʿūlū* | It gives the ground **in the direction of narrowing** |

**So the sentence structure of the verse produces more restriction than permission.** This is a countable fact: the sentence contains one command, two conditions and one ground.

### What this says to the present

**It is possible to write the bearing of this verse on the present without entering current debates and without scolding the reader; what is treated is the measure the verse itself establishes.**

**One. The threshold drawn back to fear.** The verse does not say "if you commit injustice", it says **"if you fear you will commit injustice"**. This is a rare threshold in law: not the act but the anxiety about the act is made the measure. The decision whether one can carry an obligation is left to the person's own foresight.

**Two. Responsibility tied to number.** The logic of the verse is this: as the number of relationships undertaken rises, the share of justice falling to each one falls. **This logic works independently of marriage too** — a child cared for, a job taken on, a promise given. When capacity is exceeded, the party beyond the limit is the one who suffers.

**Three. And a limit.** No general conclusion such as "a person sets his own measure" follows from this verse; the verse speaks within a particular field of ruling. And no judgement about the morals of a person or a society today can be built from here.

---

## 4/4 — وآتوا النساء صدقاتهن نحلة فإن طبن لكم عن شيء منه نفسا فكلوه هنيئا مريئا

*Wa ātu'l-nisāʾa ṣaduqātihinna niḥla, fa-in ṭibna lakum ʿan shayʾin minhu nafsan fa-kulūhu hanīʾan marīʾā*

"Give the women their bridal gifts with a glad heart. If of their own accord they leave you some part of it, then eat it with pleasure and ease."

### The repetition of the verb

**The second verse began *wa ātu'l-yatāmā amwālahum*. The fourth verse begins *wa ātu'l-nisāʾa ṣaduqātihinna*.**

**The same verb, the same pattern, two verses apart, for two different parties addressed.** This is a countable fact.

**And in both sentences the object is named as belonging to the recipient:** *amwāla**hum*** (their property) / *ṣaduqāti**hinna*** (their bridal gifts). **In both, the pronoun is on the receiving side.**

I record this as **my own reading**: the sūra gives back with the same verb what can be taken from the hands of two different groups, and it names both acts as a **restitution**.

### صَدُقَات — the root ص-د-ق

**The word the Qurʾān uses for the bridal gift comes from the root of *truthfulness*.**

**ص-د-ق:** *ṣidq* — truthfulness; *ṣādiq* — truthful; *ṣadaqa* — a gift given; *taṣdīq* — confirming as true. The root was treated in `049-hucurat.md` and in `002-bakara.md`.

**And the explanation the lexicographers record for this word is this:** the bridal gift is what shows **the seriousness and the truthfulness** of the desire to marry; the sign that there is something backing the word. **I relate this explanation in the language of "what the lexicographers record".**

**For the same thing the Qurʾān elsewhere uses the words *ajr* (25, 24) and *farīḍa* (24) as well.** The variety of words is worth recording: the same legal element is named from three different sides — a token of truthfulness, a return, an appointed share.

### نِحْلَة — the disagreement over the word

**نِحْلَة — root ن-ح-ل.** The lexicographers explain the word as **a gift given without return, given from the heart**. From the same root comes **نَحْل** (*naḥl*) — the bee; and the link the lexicographers make is this: **the bee gives back more than it takes, expecting no return.** This link is widely transmitted among the lexicographers; **I do not present it as a settled derivation.**

**There is disagreement over the word's case and meaning in the verse:**

| Reading | What *niḥla* is | Its consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Farīḍa** — an appointed, obligatory duty | The bridal gift is a debt, not a favour |
| **2** | **Hiba / ʿaṭiyya** — given from the heart, without return | The manner of giving is being regulated: not grudgingly |
| **3** | **Diyānatan** — as a religious requirement | The source of the obligation is being stressed |

**I do not impose a preference and I issue no legal ruling.**

**But the outcome common to the three readings can be recorded:** in none of them is the bridal gift a price paid to someone other than the woman. **The party addressed by the verse is the men, the recipient is the women, and there is no third party in between.**

### The historical background of this verse

**It is transmitted in the sources that in that period the bridal gift was given to the woman's guardian or withheld by him.** It is said that the verse ties the payment directly to the woman.

**I relate this in the language of "it is transmitted" and give no name of a person or a tribe.** What the wording of the verse says is clear in any case: the object of the verb is *al-nisāʾ*.

### فَإِن طِبْنَ لَكُمْ عَن شَىْءٍ مِّنْهُ نَفْسًا — the condition of consent

**In the sentence, consent is reinforced by three separate elements, and this must be recorded:**

| Element | What it does |
|---|---|
| **طِبْنَ** (*ṭibna*) | Root **ط-ي-ب** — to be pleasant, for the heart to be glad. The subject of the verb is **the women** (feminine plural) |
| **عَن شَىْءٍ مِّنْهُ** | "**Some** of it" — not the whole, but a part |
| **نَفْسًا** | Specification (*tamyīz*): "**nafsan**" — from within themselves, of their own accord |

**Read together, the three settle by whom and in what manner the consent must be given.** Without the word *nafsan* the sentence could have been read as "if they leave it to you"; the word adds the qualification that the leaving must come from **inward willingness**.

**I record this as an observation about word order;** its ground is the specification itself.

**And this is the word *nafs*'s second occurrence in the sūra:** in the first verse as the root of creation (*min nafsin wāḥida*), here as the source of consent. The same word, at two separate tasks.

### هَنِيٓـًٔا مَّرِيٓـًٔا — two adjectives

**هَنِيء — root ه-ن-أ:** pleasant, wholesome, free of trouble. **مَرِيء — root م-ر-أ:** easily digested, lying light on the stomach. (*Marʾ* — a human being; *imraʾa* — woman is counted in the same root family.)

**The distinction the lexicographers draw is worth recording:** *hanīʾ* is what is pleasant while being eaten, *marīʾ* what causes no discomfort after it has been eaten. **The first adjective therefore looks to the moment, the second to what follows.**

**And the placing of the two here yields an observation, which I record as my own reading:** the verse says that what is given with consent **will not turn into a burden of guilt afterwards** either. The picture used in the tenth verse for those who consume the orphan's property wrongfully (*innamā yaʾkulūna fī buṭūnihim nārā*) and these two adjectives stand opposite one another in the same sūra.

---

## 4/5 — ولا تؤتوا السفهاء أموالكم التي جعل الله لكم قياما وارزقوهم فيها واكسوهم وقولوا لهم قولا معروفا

*Wa lā tuʾtu'l-sufahāʾa amwālakumu'llatī jaʿala'llāhu lakum qiyāman wa'rzuqūhum fīhā wa'ksūhum wa qūlū lahum qawlan maʿrūfā*

"Do not give over to those who lack judgement the property that God has made a means of standing upright for you. Feed them from it, clothe them, and speak to them a kindly word."

### The third repetition of the verb — and its reversal

**The second verse: *wa ātu'l-yatāmā*. The fourth verse: *wa ātu'l-nisāʾa*. The fifth verse: *wa lā tuʾtu'l-sufahāʾa*.**

**The same verb a third time; but this time in the negative.** This is a weave of word order that can be verified, and it shows this: **the sūra uses the same verb when it says "give" and when it says "do not give".** The measure is not whose the property is; it is **who is able to hold it**.

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the sameness of the verb in the three verses.

### سُفَهَآء — the root س-ف-ه

**The concrete sense of the root: lightness, having no weight.** *Safihati'l-rīḥ* — the wind tossed the air about; *thawbun safīh* — cloth of loose, light weave.

**So *safah* is not the absence of intellect but the lightness of its weight.** The opposite of the word is *ḥilm* — gravity, being weighty.

The root was treated at `072-cin.md` 72/4 (*wa annahū kāna yaqūlu safīhunā ʿala'llāhi shaṭaṭā*); I rely on that treatment.

**And there is a classical disagreement over whom the word *sufahāʾ* means here:**

| View | Who they are |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Orphans** not in a position to protect their property — the context supports this |
| **2** | **Anyone** who squanders property — no condition of age |
| **3** | Not a definite group; an **attribute** is being described: whoever cannot hold property in his hands |

**I do not impose a preference.** The third reading is in keeping with the method's measure that "what the verse describes is an attribute"; but I give this as a record, not as a preference.

### أَمْوَٰلَكُمُ — the surprise in the pronoun

**Speaking of the orphan's property, the verse says *"your property"*.**

**This is a point that has been dwelt upon, and several explanations are transmitted in the classical commentaries:**

| Explanation | Its ground |
|---|---|
| **A. Having it in hand** | The property is in fact in your hands; the address is made to the one who holds the disposal of it |
| **B. The common wealth of the society** | Property is the means by which the community, not single individuals, stands upright; hence it is named with a plural pronoun of ownership |
| **C. A generic pronoun** | The genus "property" is meant; it states no particular ownership |

**I do not impose a preference.**

**But the connection of reading B with the continuation of the verse must be recorded:** the sentence goes on *allatī jaʿala'llāhu lakum qiyāman* — "which God has made a means of standing upright for you". **The property, that is, is named by its function and not by its owner.**

### قِيَٰمًا — and the weave of roots in the sūra

**قِيَٰم — root ق-و-م: to stand upright, to be set up.** *Qiyām* — the post on which a thing stands, a support. The root was treated in many places in this commentary (`002-bakara.md`, `033-ahzab.md`, `049-hucurat.md`).

**And this root occurs in three critical places in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ. This is a countable datum of the text:**

| Verse | Word | What is held upright |
|---|---|---|
| **5** | *qiyāmā* | **Property** — the means by which the community stands |
| **34** | *qawwāmūn* | **Responsibility within the family** — together with the verse's own stated ground |
| **135** | *qawwāmīna bi'l-qisṭ* | **Justice** — even against oneself |

**Read together, the three verses show the function of the root in the sūra: "to hold upright".** This table will be returned to in the treatment of the thirty-fourth verse.

I record this as **my own reading**; the places of the words can be counted in the text.

### وَٱرْزُقُوهُمْ فِيهَا — the choice of preposition

**The verse does not say *minhā* (from it), it says *fīhā* (within it).**

**In the classical commentaries the following sense is drawn from this preposition, and I relate it as it is transmitted:** provide for them not by diminishing the property but by **working** it — that is, not from the capital but from the income that arises within it. The property itself stays; the maintenance comes out of what it yields.

**This explanation is not a strict grammatical necessity**; it has also been said that the preposition *fī* stands here for *min*. **I do not impose a preference.** But it can be recorded that the explanation is in keeping with the whole of the verse: the sixth verse will command that the property be **preserved and handed over**.

### وَقُولُوا۟ لَهُمْ قَوْلًا مَّعْرُوفًا — the third command

**The verse commands three things, and the third is worth recording:** feed them, clothe them, **and speak a kindly word.**

**Beside the decision not to hand over property, that is, is set how the tongue is to be used.** A qualification is entered upon the party that holds the authority, concerning the sentence he frames while exercising it.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the order of the commands:** immediately after commanding the restriction, the verse commands how one is to speak with the person restricted.

**And the same expression will come once more in the eighth verse: *wa qūlū lahum qawlan maʿrūfā*.** Two verses apart, the same sentence for two different groups. **In both, the party addressed is the one left empty-handed.**

The same field was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/83 (*wa qūlū li'l-nāsi ḥusnā*) and at 2/263 (*qawlun maʿrūfun wa maghfiratun khayrun min ṣadaqatin yatbaʿuhā adhā*); I rely on those treatments.

---

## 4/6 — وابتلوا اليتامى حتى إذا بلغوا النكاح فإن آنستم منهم رشدا فادفعوا إليهم أموالهم ولا تأكلوها إسرافا وبدارا أن يكبروا ومن كان غنيا فليستعفف ومن كان فقيرا فليأكل بالمعروف فإذا دفعتم إليهم أموالهم فأشهدوا عليهم وكفى بالله حسيبا

*Wa'btalu'l-yatāmā ḥattā idhā balaghu'l-nikāḥ, fa-in ānastum minhum rushdan fa'dfaʿū ilayhim amwālahum, wa lā taʾkulūhā isrāfan wa bidāran an yakbarū, wa man kāna ghaniyyan fa'l-yastaʿfif wa man kāna faqīran fa'l-yaʾkul bi'l-maʿrūf, fa-idhā dafaʿtum ilayhim amwālahum fa-ashhidū ʿalayhim, wa kafā bi'llāhi ḥasībā*

"Test the orphans until they reach the age of marriage. If you perceive in them a soundness of judgement, hand over their property to them. Do not consume it wastefully, in haste before they grow up. Let the one who is rich abstain; let the one who is poor consume in the recognised measure. When you hand over their property to them, call witnesses. God suffices as a reckoner."

### What the verse does: it sets up a process

**The fifth verse forbade handing over the property. The sixth verse regulates when the prohibition ends.** Read apart, the fifth verse looks like a permanent restriction; read together with the sixth, it is seen to be the regulation of a **period of transition**.

**The sequence the verse establishes is this, and it can be verified:**

| Step | The expression in the verse | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *Wa'btalū* | **Test them** |
| **2** | *Ḥattā idhā balaghu'l-nikāḥ* | **A measure of time**: the age of marriage |
| **3** | *Fa-in ānastum minhum rushdā* | **A measure of quality**: that soundness be perceived |
| **4** | *Fa'dfaʿū ilayhim amwālahum* | **Handing over** |
| **5** | *Fa-ashhidū ʿalayhim* | **Calling witnesses** |

**Of the five steps, two are measures, one is an act, two are procedures.** And the two measures are set together: **age alone is not enough, and soundness alone is not named either.**

### وَٱبْتَلُوا۟ — the root ب-ل-و

**The concrete sense of the root: to wear something out by using it.** To wear out a garment by wearing it. And from there comes the sense of **testing**: what a thing is comes out only when it is used.

The root was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/155 and at `029-ankebut.md` 29/2; I rely on those treatments.

**And the placing of the root here is worth recording:** the verse does not say "look", "ask", "observe" — it says **"test"**. **Soundness is established not by opinion but by trial.**

**How the testing is to be done is not written in the verse.** In classical jurisprudence there are detailed views on this (having them make small purchases, giving them responsibility for spending); **those details are not entered into in this section, and no ruling is issued.**

### ءَانَسْتُم — the root أ-ن-س

**Root أ-ن-س: to notice something with familiarity, from close at hand.** *Uns* — closeness, familiarity; *insān* — is connected with this root (in `033-ahzab.md` it was recorded that nothing definite is said about the root of the word; I keep the same record here).

The root was treated at `024-nur.md` 24/27 (*ḥattā tastaʾnisū*); I rely on that treatment.

**And the choice of verb is worth recording:** the verse does not say *raʾaytum* (you saw) or *ʿalimtum* (you knew). *Ānastum* — **you noticed, you sensed.** The word states recognition rather than certain knowledge.

**The Qurʾān uses the same verb for Moses's (Mūsā's) noticing the fire** (*innī ānastu nāran* — Ṭāhā 20/10; treated in `020-taha.md`). **In both places there is the sensing of a sign from a distance.**

### رُشْد — the root ر-ش-د

**Root ر-ش-د: that a road be straight, that it lead to the goal.** *Rāshid* — one on the right road; *irshād* — showing the way. The opposite of the word is *ghayy* (losing one's way).

**And there is a classical disagreement over what *rushd* is here:**

| View | What *rushd* is |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Competence in property** — the capacity to protect and manage property |
| **2** | **Religious maturity** — the ability to carry religious responsibility |
| **3** | **The two together** — competence both in property and in character |

**This disagreement produced a wide discussion in classical jurisprudence, and its consequences differ among the schools of law. I do not impose a preference, I issue no legal ruling, and none of them is binding.**

**What follows from the wording of the verse and is not in dispute is this:** the word comes **indefinite** (*rushdan* — "a soundness"), that is, what is sought is not a complete and flawless maturity but **a soundness that can be perceived**.

### إِسْرَافًا وَبِدَارًا أَن يَكْبَرُوا۟ — two states

**Two verbal nouns come side by side, and both describe the manner in which the consuming is done:**

| Word | Root | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **إِسْرَاف** | **س-ر-ف** | To exceed the measure, to squander |
| **بِدَار** | **ب-د-ر** | To race, to get ahead, to hurry |

**The concrete sense of the root ب-د-ر is worth recording:** *badr* — the full moon. The lexicographers explain this by the root's sense of "to get ahead, to arrive first". *Bādara* — to hasten to a task.

**And the picture in the expression *bidāran an yakbarū* must be recorded: "racing against their growing up".** The orphan will grow and ask for his property; the guardian moves before that. **The verse describes a race run against time.**

**This matches the concrete sense of the word exactly**, and I record it as an observation about a word.

### وَمَن كَانَ غَنِيًّا فَلْيَسْتَعْفِفْ — the wealthy guardian

**فَلْيَسْتَعْفِفْ — root ع-ف-ف, Form X.** *ʿIffa* — to hold oneself back, to keep away. Form X here states **keeping away deliberately and with effort**.

**What the sentence does must be recorded:** where the guardian is wealthy, no prohibition is laid down — **he is asked to hold himself back.** The ruling, that is, varies with the person's own circumstances.

### وَمَن كَانَ فَقِيرًا فَلْيَأْكُلْ بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ — the poor guardian

**And there is a classical disagreement over the scope of this clause:**

| View | What eating *bi'l-maʿrūf* is |
|---|---|
| **1** | To take a **wage** in return for his labour |
| **2** | To take what necessity requires and then **repay** it (counted a debt) |
| **3** | To take what necessity requires and **not repay** it |

**This disagreement is real in classical jurisprudence, and the schools divide. I do not impose a preference, I issue no legal ruling, and none of them is binding.**

**The qualification the verse itself lays down must be recorded: *bi'l-maʿrūf*.** The root **ع-ر-ف** — known, recognised, accepted by custom. **The amount, that is, is not fixed; the measure is the measure known in that community.**

**The word occurs very frequently in this sūra** (5, 6, 8, 19, 25, 114) and almost every time it draws the **limit** of a ruling. This is a countable fact.

### فَأَشْهِدُوا۟ عَلَيْهِمْ — that the handing over be done before witnesses

**The verse ties the handing over to a procedure: the calling of witnesses.**

**And the logic of this runs in two directions, and I record it as my own reading:** the witness protects both the one who receives the property and the one who hands it over. The orphan cannot say "it was never given to me", nor is the guardian left without proof of his claim "I gave it". **Witnessing, that is, is not an act of mistrust but a way of protecting the relationship from a future dispute.**

The same logic was treated in detail at `002-bakara.md` 2/282 (the writing down of a debt); I rely on that treatment.

### وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ حَسِيبًا — the verse-ending

**حَسِيب — root ح-س-ب: to count, to reckon.** *Ḥisāb* — a reckoning; *ḥasb* — sufficiency ("this is enough for me"). **The two senses of the root — to count and to suffice — meet in one place:** what is counted becomes sufficient when it is complete.

**And the pattern of the verse-ending must be recorded: *kafā bi'llāhi …* — "God suffices as …".** This pattern recurs several times in this sūra (6, 45, 70, 79, 81, 132, 166, 171) and six different attributes follow it: *ḥasībā*, *waliyyan*, *naṣīrā*, *ʿalīmā*, *wakīlā*, *shahīdā*.

**This is an observation about style, and the places of the words can be counted.** Having laid down the obligation, the sūra points again and again to the same place.

---

## 4/7 — للرجال نصيب مما ترك الوالدان والأقربون وللنساء نصيب مما ترك الوالدان والأقربون مما قل منه أو كثر نصيبا مفروضا

*Li'l-rijāli naṣībun mimmā taraka'l-wālidāni wa'l-aqrabūn, wa li'l-nisāʾi naṣībun mimmā taraka'l-wālidāni wa'l-aqrabūna mimmā qalla minhu aw kathur, naṣīban mafrūḍā*

"To the men belongs a share of what parents and near kin leave; and to the women belongs a share of what parents and near kin leave — of the little of it and of the much. An appointed share."

### The building of the sentence twice

**The most striking side of the verse is that the same sentence is repeated twice, with not one of its words abbreviated.**

| | The first sentence | The second sentence |
|---|---|---|
| **To whom** | *Li'l-rijāli* | *Wa li'l-nisāʾi* |
| **What** | *naṣībun* | *naṣībun* |
| **From what** | *mimmā taraka'l-wālidāni wa'l-aqrabūn* | *mimmā taraka'l-wālidāni wa'l-aqrabūn* |

**In Arabic this repetition is not required.** The sentence could have been built once: "to the men and to the women there is a share". **It was not.**

**I record this as an observation about word order, and its ground is the repetition itself:** that the right is tied to each of the two parties separately, in a complete sentence, is said by the repetition itself. The same technique will be used once more in the thirty-second verse (*li'l-rijāli naṣībun mimma'ktasabū wa li'l-nisāʾi naṣībun mimma'ktasabna*), and will be compared there in a table.

**A similar repetition was treated at `033-ahzab.md` 33/35** — there ten pairs of attributes were counted out one by one, and in that discussion it was recorded **as my own reading** that "the repetition itself carries information". **I keep the same record here: this is an observation, not a transmitted report.**

### نَصِيب — the root ن-ص-ب

**The concrete sense of the root: to plant something, to set it standing upright.** *Naṣb* — to set up; *nuṣub* — an erected stone; *naṣab* — weariness (setting oneself up, wearing oneself out). The root was treated at `094-insirah.md` 94/7 (*fa-idhā faraghta fa'nṣab*) and at `088-gasiye.md` 88/3.

**And that the word *naṣīb* comes from this root is worth recording:** a share is what has been **set up** — put in its place, fixed. The word carries "being determined" within itself.

**The verse says this separately as well: *naṣīban mafrūḍā*.**

### مَّفْرُوضًا — the root ف-ر-ض

**Root ف-ر-ض: to notch, to cut a mark.** To fix the measure of something by opening a mark upon it. The root was treated in detail at `024-nur.md` 24/1 (*faraḍnāhā*); I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it here.

**The record made there sits directly here:** *farḍ* is not "a heavy obligation" but **a notched measure**. What is not left indefinite.

**And the use of the two words together is an emphasis: *naṣīban mafrūḍā*** — "a share set up and notched". **Both words state fixity.**

### مِمَّا قَلَّ مِنْهُ أَوْ كَثُرَ — the amount rendered immaterial

**The sentence removes the size of the estate from the ruling: "of the little of it and of the much."**

**I record this as an observation:** the coming into being of the right is not tied to the amount. It cannot be said of a small estate that "nothing will come out of it anyway".

### Historical background

**It is transmitted in the sources that in that period the estate was given to the men who could fight and ride, and that women and small children took no share.** It is also transmitted that in some places the woman herself was counted as **an object of inheritance** — and this will be forbidden outright in the nineteenth verse (*lā yaḥillu lakum an tarithu'l-nisāʾa karhā*).

**I relate this in the language of "it is transmitted"; I give no name of a person or a tribe.**

**And the function of the verse, as it can be read from the text, is this:** before the figures of the shares are given in the eleventh verse, **the right itself** is proclaimed. The seventh verse is the principle, the eleventh its reckoning.

---

## 4/8 — وإذا حضر القسمة أولو القربى واليتامى والمساكين فارزقوهم منه وقولوا لهم قولا معروفا

*Wa idhā ḥaḍara'l-qismata ulu'l-qurbā wa'l-yatāmā wa'l-masākīnu fa'rzuqūhum minhu wa qūlū lahum qawlan maʿrūfā*

"If relatives, orphans and the poor are present at the division, give them something out of it, and speak to them a kindly word."

### A verse set among the verses of inheritance

**The seventh verse said that the share exists; the eleventh will give the shares. The eighth verse stands between the two and speaks of *those who have no share*.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the position of the verse:** before it works out the reckoning of the right, the sūra names those who fall outside the reckoning. **A field is left outside the law.**

The same structure was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/237: the leaving of a field beyond the share the law assigns (*wa lā tansawu'l-faḍla baynakum*). **I rely on that treatment.** What the two verses have in common is this: **a ruling is laid down, and then a door is opened beyond the ruling.**

### Who is counted

| Group | Who | Share of the estate |
|---|---|---|
| **أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْقُرْبَىٰ** | Relatives — but those who take no share | None |
| **ٱلْيَتَٰمَىٰ** | Orphans | None in this context |
| **ٱلْمَسَٰكِين** | The poor | None |

**What the three have in common: they are there at the division but are not included in the reckoning.**

**مِسْكِين — root س-ك-ن:** stillness, absence of movement. *Sakana* — it came to rest; *sakīna* — calm, tranquillity; *maskana* — the immobilised condition of poverty. **The lexicographers record the picture of the word thus:** the person whom poverty itself has made unable to move from his place. **The word was treated in `090-beled.md` and `107-maun.md`.**

### The disagreement over the character of the ruling

**Whether this verse is a binding ruling or a recommendation has been debated in classical exegesis:**

| View | Its ground |
|---|---|
| **A binding ruling** | The imperative mood (*fa'rzuqūhum*) |
| **Recommended (*mandūb*)** | That no amount is specified; that the verse is not named together with the shares of inheritance |
| **That it has lapsed** | The view that the ruling changed when the verses of inheritance came later |

**This disagreement is real and its outcome differs among the schools of law. I do not impose a preference, I issue no legal ruling, and none of them is binding.**

**What follows from the wording of the verse and is not in dispute:** no amount is stated, and **a word** is added beside the command.

### وَقُولُوا۟ لَهُمْ قَوْلًا مَّعْرُوفًا — a second time

**The same sentence occurred in the fifth verse too. In both verses the party addressed is the one left empty-handed.**

**And its function here is worth recording:** when nothing is given at a division, or too little is given, what breaks the relationship is most often not the amount but the word that is spoken. **The verse sets the word to be spoken beside the thing to be given.**

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the standing of the two commands side by side in one sentence.

---

## 4/9 — وليخش الذين لو تركوا من خلفهم ذرية ضعافا خافوا عليهم فليتقوا الله وليقولوا قولا سديدا

*Wa'l-yakhsha'lladhīna law tarakū min khalfihim dhurriyyatan ḍiʿāfan khāfū ʿalayhim, fa'l-yattaqu'llāha wa'l-yaqūlū qawlan sadīdā*

"Let those be wary who, if they left weak offspring behind them, would fear for them. Let them be mindful of God and speak a word that is right / that holds."

### The mirror the verse sets up

**This verse uses one of the plainest ethical techniques in the Qurʾān, and the name of the technique is exchange of places.**

The structure of the sentence is this: *"those who, if they themselves left weak offspring behind them, would fear for them…"* — **that is, the one addressed is told to put his own child in the place of the orphan before him.**
