**And this is not an appeal to feeling but an appeal to reasoning.** The verse does not say "have pity"; it says **"consider your own situation"**. The difference between the two is this: pity depends on the state of the other party and shifts; **exchange of places is a measure, and it is constant.**

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the construction of the conditional clause (*law tarakū … khāfū ʿalayhim*).

### The difference between خَشْيَة and خَوْف

**The verse uses two different roots in the same sentence, and this must be recorded.**

| Root | Word | The distinction the lexicographers record |
|---|---|---|
| **خ-ش-ي** | *Wa'l-yakhsha* (imperative) | A wariness grounded in knowledge, mixed with reverence. The Qurʾān mostly uses it of God |
| **خ-و-ف** | *Khāfū* (perfect) | Fear arising from a harm; general |

**And their places in the word order are meaningful:** *khashya* is commanded of the one addressed, while *khawf* is ascribed to the hypothetical father.

**I record this as an observation about word order:** the sentence takes the natural fear a man feels for his own child (*khawf*) and turns it into the deliberate wariness that ought to be felt for another's child (*khashya*).

### ذُرِّيَّةً ضِعَٰفًا — the journey of the word through the sūra

**ضِعَاف — root ض-ع-ف: weakness.** And this root will return in a far heavier place in the later parts of the sūra:

| Verse | Word | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **9** | *ḍiʿāfan* | The **children** left behind |
| **75** | *al-mustaḍʿafīna mina'l-rijāli wa'l-nisāʾi wa'l-wildān* | Those **made** weak |
| **98** | *al-mustaḍʿafīna …* | Those unable to migrate |
| **127** | *al-mustaḍʿafīna mina'l-wildān* | Children whose rights are not given them |

**In the ninth verse weakness is a *state*; in the seventy-fifth it passes into Form X and becomes *something that is done*.**

**This distinction was treated at `028-kasas.md` 28/4-5, and it was recorded there:** *"**يَسْتَضْعِفُ** — root **ض-ع-ف**, Form X: to reckon someone weak and to make him weak. The pattern matters: weakness is not something that happens of itself but something that is *done*."* **I rely on that treatment.**

**This weave of roots is one of the clearest data showing the backbone of the sūra, and the places of the words can be counted.**

### قَوْلًا سَدِيدًا — the root س-د-د

**Root س-د-د: to close a breach, to stop it up; and to aim straight, to hit the mark.** *Sadd* — a barrier, a dam (a wall that closes a gap); *sadād* — hitting the mark; *musaddad* — aimed at a target.

**So *qawlan sadīdan* says two things at once:** a word that hits the mark **and** a word that closes a gap.

**The same expression occurs at `033-ahzab.md` 33/70** (*yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanu'ttaqu'llāha wa qūlū qawlan sadīdā*) **and was treated there; I rely on that treatment.**

**Comparing the two verses is worth recording:**

| Place | The word spoken, and for whom |
|---|---|
| al-Aḥzāb 33/70 | General — speech itself is being regulated |
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/9** | **Speech in the setting where an orphan is present** |

**And the context here makes the sense of the word precise:** the word spoken at the division of an estate and at the making of a bequest. **What the verse asks for, then, is a word that is both true and leaves no gap.**

### The disagreement over whom the verse addresses

**Several views are transmitted in the classical commentaries about whom this verse speaks to:**

| View | Those addressed |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Guardians** — those who hold the orphan's property |
| **2** | **Those at the bedside of the dying** — who urge a man to bequeath his property to someone else; the verse warns them |
| **3** | **The one making the bequest** — that he not bequeath so much as to leave his heirs destitute |

**I do not impose a preference.** All three are compatible with the wording of the verse, and all three come out at the same place: **that the right of the one left behind be looked after.**

---

## 4/10 — إن الذين يأكلون أموال اليتامى ظلما إنما يأكلون في بطونهم نارا وسيصلون سعيرا

*Inna'lladhīna yaʾkulūna amwāla'l-yatāmā ẓulman innamā yaʾkulūna fī buṭūnihim nārā, wa sayaṣlawna saʿīrā*

"Those who consume the property of orphans wrongfully consume nothing but fire into their bellies; and they will enter a blazing fire."

### The closing of the block

**The orphan block (2-10) closes here, and the closing is the harshest sentence in the block.**

**The course of the block is worth recording:**

| Verse | Language |
|---|---|
| 2 | Command and prohibition; verse-ending: *ḥūban kabīrā* |
| 3-4 | Ruling and condition |
| 5-6 | Process and measure |
| 7-8 | The right and what lies beyond the right |
| 9 | The call to change places |
| **10** | **The outcome: fire** |

**The block, that is, begins with regulation, continues with reasoning and ends with sanction.** This is a countable sequence.

### ظُلْمًا — the place of the qualification

**The verse does not say "those who consume the orphan's property"; it says *"those who consume it wrongfully"*.**

**This qualification is necessary, because the sixth verse permitted the poor guardian to consume *bi'l-maʿrūf*.** The word *ẓulman* keeps the two verses apart.

**The concrete sense of the root ظ-ل-م: to put a thing somewhere other than its place.** The root was treated in `002-bakara.md` and at `031-lokman.md` 31/13; I rely on those treatments.

**And this sense of the root matches the subject of the verse exactly:** the orphan's property is put somewhere other than where it belongs.

### إِنَّمَا يَأْكُلُونَ فِى بُطُونِهِمْ نَارًا — the building of the picture

**There are three elements in the sentence, and the three work together:**

| Element | What it does |
|---|---|
| **إِنَّمَا** | The particle of restriction: "nothing but, only". It says that what they consume **is nothing else** |
| **فِى بُطُونِهِمْ** | "Into their bellies" — where the act goes is stated openly |
| **نَارًا** | The name of what is eaten: **fire** |

**What the sentence does is to recount a future punishment in the present tense.** The verb is imperfect (*yaʾkulūn*) — it is happening now. The punishment is stated separately and in the future: *wa **sayaṣlawna** saʿīrā.*

**Two separate things, then, are being said:** one that is happening now (what is eaten is fire), the other that will happen later (they will enter the fire).

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the moods of the two verbs.

### A picture shared with al-Baqara

**The same picture was set up at `002-bakara.md` 2/174:** *"Inna'lladhīna yaktumūna mā anzala'llāhu mina'l-kitābi… **ulāʾika mā yaʾkulūna fī buṭūnihim illa'l-nār**"* — there the heading of the section was **"Those who fill their bellies with fire"**. **I rely on that treatment.**

**Comparing the two verses is worth recording:**

| Place | Who | What they do |
|---|---|---|
| al-Baqara 2/174 | Those who conceal something of the Book and take a small price for it | They turn **knowledge** into money |
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/10** | Those who consume the orphan's property wrongfully | They turn **a trust** into property |

**In both, the same verb (*yaʾkulūna fī buṭūnihim*), the same object (*nār*).** This is a shared expression between two sūras that can be verified.

**And the point in common is this, which I record as my own reading:** in both verses a benefit is obtained in exchange for something that ought to have been protected. **The Qurʾān names these two acts with the same sentence.**

### سَعِير — the root س-ع-ر

**Root س-ع-ر: the kindling and flaring up of a fire.** *Saʿara'l-nār* — he kindled the fire; *mustaʿir* — set ablaze. The root was treated in `067-mulk.md` and `054-kamer.md`.

**And the verb *sayaṣlawna* — root ص-ل-ي:** to enter the fire, to burn in the fire. The word occurred in `069-hakka.md`, `074-muddessir.md` and `084-insikak.md`.

### What this says to the present

**The bearing of this block (2-10) on the present can be written without accusing anyone; what is treated is the structure the block establishes.**

**One. The most fragile form of a trust.** From beginning to end the block regulates one situation: **that property is in someone else's hands while its owner cannot defend it.** This situation is not peculiar to the orphan's property — agency, guardianship, holding an office, using an institution's funds all have the same structure. The measures the block lays down (not mixing, testing, handing over, calling witnesses) fit that whole structure.

**Two. The measure of changing places.** The mirror set up by the ninth verse is still one of the most workable instruments of moral reasoning today: **if your own child were in the same position.** The verse puts this not as a feeling but as a method of reckoning.

**Three. And a limit.** It does not follow from this block that everyone who holds property in his hands is under suspicion. The sixth verse lays down two separate rulings according to the guardian's circumstances and opens a way for the one who is poor. **The block does not do away with trust; it asks that trust be open to examination.**

---

## 4/11 — يوصيكم الله في أولادكم للذكر مثل حظ الأنثيين … من بعد وصية يوصي بها أو دين

*Yūṣīkumu'llāhu fī awlādikum li'l-dhakari mithlu ḥaẓẓi'l-unthayayn, fa-in kunna nisāʾan fawqa'thnatayni fa-lahunna thuluthā mā tarak, wa in kānat wāḥidatan fa-laha'l-niṣf, wa li-abawayhi li-kulli wāḥidin minhuma'l-sudusu mimmā taraka in kāna lahū walad, fa-in lam yakun lahū waladun wa warithahū abawāhu fa-li-ummihi'l-thuluth, fa-in kāna lahū ikhwatun fa-li-ummihi'l-sudus, min baʿdi waṣiyyatin yūṣī bihā aw dayn, ābāʾukum wa abnāʾukum lā tadrūna ayyuhum aqrabu lakum nafʿā, farīḍatan mina'llāh, inna'llāha kāna ʿAlīman Ḥakīmā*

"God enjoins upon you concerning your children: to the male the like of the share of two females. If they are women more than two, theirs is two-thirds of what is left; if she is one alone, hers is a half. To his parents, to each one of them, falls a sixth of what he leaves, if he has a child. If he has no child and his parents are his heirs, a third falls to his mother. If he has brothers, a sixth falls to his mother. All this after any bequest made and after any debt. Your fathers and your sons — you do not know which of them is nearer to you in benefit. This is an ordinance from God. God is Knowing, Wise."

### First, a note on method — and this note holds for the whole of the eleventh, twelfth and hundred and seventy-sixth verses

**Inheritance is the most technical discussion in classical jurisprudence, and it produced a discipline of its own (*farāʾiḍ*).**

**What will be done in this section is this:**
1. **The shares given by the verse's own wording** are set out in a table.
2. **The terms are analysed**: *ḥaẓẓ*, *waṣiyya*, *dayn*, *kalāla*, *farīḍa*.
3. **The order of priority the verse itself lays down** is brought to the fore: *min baʿdi waṣiyyatin yūṣī bihā aw dayn*.

**And these will not be done:**
- **The debates over reckoning will not be entered into.** What is to be done when the shares do not reach one whole or exceed it (the matters known in the classical literature as *ʿawliyya* and *raddiyya*), which heir is excluded in which case, the position of the grandfather and of the brothers — none of these is the subject of this text.
- **No legal ruling will be issued and no preference will be imposed.**
- **This is not a guide to practice.** The division of an actual estate belongs to those competent in it and to the relevant law.

**And this record is entered plainly: inheritance is a subject of jurisprudence in which there are differences of detail among the schools of law; nothing related in this section is binding.**

### يُوصِيكُمُ ٱللَّهُ — the choice of verb

**The verse does not say *yaḥkumu* (He rules) or *yaʾmurukum* (He commands you). It says *Yūṣīkum* — "He enjoins upon you, He charges you."**

**The concrete sense of the root و-ص-ي: to join, to attach one thing to another.** *Arḍun wāṣiya* — land whose plants have run together, contiguous ground. And from there *waṣiyya*: to **carry** a matter over to another, to attach it to him.

**The picture in the root, then, is a *joining*.** And this looks in the same direction as the word *al-arḥām* (ties) in the sūra's first verse.

**I record this as an observation about a word:** the ruling on inheritance is given not with the verb "to rule" but with a verb from the root of "joining".

**And that the verb is imperfect must be recorded: *yūṣīkum*** — ongoing. The pattern states that the ruling is not a one-time order but **a lasting arrangement**.

### حَظّ — the root ح-ظ-ظ

**For "share" the verse here uses not *naṣīb* but the word *ḥaẓẓ*.**

**The root ح-ظ-ظ: the portion, the lot that falls to a person.** The lexicographers explain the word as "the portion set aside for a person"; *maḥẓūẓ* — one whose portion is ample.

**The distribution of the two words in the sūra is worth recording:**

| Word | Where | Its context |
|---|---|---|
| **نَصِيب** (*naṣīb*) | 7 (twice), 32 (twice), 33, 118 | **The proclamation that the right exists** |
| **حَظّ** (*ḥaẓẓ*) | 11, 176 | **The determination of the amount of the share** |

**This is an observation about word order, and the places of the words can be counted.** *Naṣīb* names the principle, *ḥaẓẓ* the reckoning.

### The shares that follow from the verse's wording — a table

**The table below shows only the proportions that occur in the wording of the verse. It is not a table for practice; it does no more than arrange the text of the verse.**

| Case | Who | The proportion the verse gives |
|---|---|---|
| Children together | Male child | **Twice** that of the female child (*mithlu ḥaẓẓi'l-unthayayn*) |
| Female children only, more than two | The daughters | **Two-thirds** (*thuluthā mā tarak*) |
| A single daughter | The daughter | **A half** (*al-niṣf*) |
| If the deceased has a child | Mother and father, **each one** | **A sixth** (*al-sudus*) |
| The deceased has no child and his parents are his heirs | The mother | **A third** (*al-thuluth*) |
| If the deceased has brothers | The mother | **A sixth** (*al-sudus*) |

**Every row in the table is in the wording of the verse.** No case not found in the verse has been added to the table.

**And three things must be recorded that do not appear in the table but can be read from the wording of the verse:**

**One.** The verse names **a female heir in three separate places**: the daughter (twice), the mother (twice). **Most of the rulings, that is, are laid down in order to determine the share of a female heir.** This is a countable fact.

**Two.** The verse states **the father's share openly in one case only** (a sixth, if there is a child); in the others it does not specify it. **The mother's share, on the other hand, is stated openly in two separate cases.**

**Three.** The sentence *li'l-dhakari mithlu ḥaẓẓi'l-unthayayn* states **a proportion**, not a ranking of worth. **The thing measured in the sentence is the male's share, and that share is defined by reference to the daughter's share** — that is, in the sentence the *unit of measure* is the female share. **I record this as an observation about word order, and I draw no ruling from it.**

**There are explanations transmitted in the classical literature about the ground of this proportion** — the distribution of the obligation of maintenance, the obligation of the bridal gift, financial responsibilities within the family, and the like. **These explanations are recorded in the language of "it is transmitted"; no preference is imposed, no ruling is established, and current debates are not entered into.** The verse itself gives no clause of justification for this proportion.

### مِنۢ بَعْدِ وَصِيَّةٍ يُوصِى بِهَآ أَوْ دَيْنٍ — the order of priority the verse itself lays down

**This qualification is the most often repeated sentence in the verses of inheritance, and it needs to be brought to the fore.**

**In the eleventh and twelfth verses this qualification is repeated four times**, and each time the form of the verb changes:

| Place | Form | Who makes the bequest |
|---|---|---|
| 11 | *yūṣī bihā* | The deceased (masculine singular) |
| 12 | *yūṣīna bihā* | Deceased women (feminine plural) |
| 12 | *tūṣūna bihā* | Those addressed (second person plural) |
| 12 | *yūṣā bihā* | Passive form — who is not specified |

**Four repetitions are a datum of the text.**

**And the sense of the qualification is this:** before the shares are reckoned, **two things are taken out** — any bequest made and any debt. **The estate itself, that is, is what remains.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the repetition itself:** while giving the most technical of reckonings, the verse recalls four times what comes **before** the reckoning. The right of the one who has a share arises after the obligations the deceased has left behind.

**It is recorded in the classical literature that the debt comes before the bequest**, although in the verse they are joined by the particle *aw* (or). **This is a question of jurisprudence, and no ruling is issued here.** What the wording of the verse says is that both come **before** the shares.

### دَيْن — the root د-ي-ن

**Root د-ي-ن.** And the family of words the lexicographers record is worth noting: *dayn* — debt; *dāna* — he became indebted, he submitted; *dīn* — religion, and at the same time "judgement, recompense, obedience"; *madīna* — a city (a place bound to an order); *dayyān* — the one who calls to account.

**The common core is this, and the lexicographers record it: to be bound by a tie, to be under something.** The debtor is under the creditor; the servant under his Lord.

**I relate this connection as the lexicographers record it, and I build no interpretation upon it.**

### وَصِيَّة — and its limit

**The bequest is named in the verse without restriction.** But in the twelfth verse a qualification is added: *ghayra muḍārr* — **not causing harm.**

**The root ض-ر-ر, Form III.** The same pattern was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/231 (*ḍirāran li-taʿtadū*), and it was recorded there: *"the pattern is Form III (*mufāʿala*): it states reciprocity or intent. That is, 'so that harm should come to her, knowingly.'"* **I rely on that treatment.**

**And the function of the qualification must be recorded:** a bequest cannot be used as an instrument for depriving an heir of his share. **The verse, that is, foresees the misuse of the very power it grants.**

**The amount of the bequest is not specified in the verse.** In classical jurisprudence a limit has been set in this matter, and its ground is transmitted as the sunna, not the verse. **This is a question of jurisprudence; no ruling is issued here and nothing is said about the amount.**

### ءَابَآؤُكُمْ وَأَبْنَآؤُكُمْ لَا تَدْرُونَ أَيُّهُمْ أَقْرَبُ لَكُمْ نَفْعًا — the position of the sentence

**This sentence stands in the very middle of a technical table of reckoning, and its bearing on the subject is not visible at first sight.**

**"Your fathers and your sons — you do not know which of them is nearer to you in benefit."**

**I record what the sentence does as my own reading, and its ground is the place where the sentence stands:**

At the division of an estate the most natural human objection is this: "he was closer", "he gave more labour", "he will bring more benefit". **The sentence is set directly in the path of that objection and ties it to a lack of knowledge.**

**The shares, that is, are given not by the benefit of persons but by a fixed measure** — because the knowledge needed to calculate benefit is not in human hands.

**And the verb must be recorded: *lā tadrūn* — "you do not know".** The root **د-ر-ي**: to know something by searching it out, by following its trace. The verb says that the knowledge **is not obtainable**.

**The pattern looks in the same direction as the pattern treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/216:** *"It may be that you dislike a thing and it is good for you… **God knows and you do not know.**"* **And it was recorded there:** *"the sentence is not a claim to knowledge but **the statement of a limit**. The place where the decision rests is not what the person sees at that moment."* **I rely on that treatment, and the same record holds here.**

### فَرِيضَةً مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ — the closing qualification

**The root ف-ر-ض occurred in the seventh verse (*naṣīban mafrūḍā*); here it comes again.** A bridge of words is built between the two verses: **the proclamation of the right and the reckoning of the right are joined by the same root.**

**And the qualification *mina'llāh* must be recorded:** the source of the measure is stated. **The shares, that is, are not a custom or a settlement but a measure that has been laid down** — this is the verse's own claim.

### إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ كَانَ عَلِيمًا حَكِيمًا — the verse-ending

**Two names side by side: *ʿAlīm* (Knowing) and *Ḥakīm* (Wise, the one who puts each thing in its place).**

**And the verse-ending's link with the verse is direct:** a few lines earlier the verse had said *lā tadrūna* — "you do not know". **The verse-ending says where the knowledge is.**

**The concrete sense of the root ح-ك-م was treated in `002-bakara.md` and at `031-lokman.md` 31/12:** *ḥakama* — to put the bit in a horse's mouth; *ḥikma* — knowledge that does not let a thing shift from its place. **I rely on that treatment.**

---

## 4/12 — ولكم نصف ما ترك أزواجكم إن لم يكن لهن ولد … وإن كان رجل يورث كلالة أو امرأة وله أخ أو أخت فلكل واحد منهما السدس

*Wa lakum niṣfu mā taraka azwājukum in lam yakun lahunna walad, fa-in kāna lahunna waladun fa-lakumu'l-rubuʿu mimmā tarakna min baʿdi waṣiyyatin yūṣīna bihā aw dayn, wa lahunna'l-rubuʿu mimmā taraktum in lam yakun lakum walad, fa-in kāna lakum waladun fa-lahunna'l-thumunu mimmā taraktum min baʿdi waṣiyyatin tūṣūna bihā aw dayn, wa in kāna rajulun yūrathu kalālatan awi'mraʾatun wa lahū akhun aw ukhtun fa-li-kulli wāḥidin minhuma'l-sudus, fa-in kānū akthara min dhālika fa-hum shurakāʾu fi'l-thuluthi min baʿdi waṣiyyatin yūṣā bihā aw daynin ghayra muḍārr, waṣiyyatan mina'llāh, wa'llāhu ʿAlīmun Ḥalīm*

"Half of what your wives leave is yours — if they have no child. If they have a child, a quarter of it is yours; after any bequest they have made and after any debt. A quarter of what you leave is theirs — if you have no child. If you have a child, an eighth of it is theirs; after any bequest you have made and after any debt. If a man or a woman leaves an estate as *kalāla* and has a brother or a sister, a sixth falls to each of them. If they are more than that, they are partners in a third; after any bequest made without causing harm and after any debt. This is a charge from God. God is Knowing, Forbearing."

### The shares that follow from the verse's wording — a table

**The note on method above holds here too: this table does no more than arrange the wording of the verse; it is not a table for practice.**

| Who | Case | The proportion the verse gives |
|---|---|---|
| **The husband** | If his wife has no child | **A half** (*niṣf*) |
| **The husband** | If his wife has a child | **A quarter** (*rubuʿ*) |
| **The wife (or wives)** | If the husband has no child | **A quarter** (*rubuʿ*) |
| **The wife (or wives)** | If the husband has a child | **An eighth** (*thumun*) |
| **A sibling (in the case of kalāla)** | If there is one brother or one sister | **A sixth to each** (*sudus*) |
| **Siblings (in the case of kalāla)** | If they are more than one | **Partners in a third** (*shurakāʾu fi'l-thuluth*) |

### The symmetry in the word order

**The verse gives the share of the wife and of the husband in the same structure, one after the other, and with the same qualifications.**

| | The husband | The wife |
|---|---|---|
| If there is no child | 1/2 | 1/4 |
| If there is a child | 1/4 | 1/8 |
| Qualification | *min baʿdi waṣiyyatin yūṣīna bihā aw dayn* | *min baʿdi waṣiyyatin tūṣūna bihā aw dayn* |

**The structure is identical, and the share of each side falls by half according to whether there is a child.** This is a countable fact.

**I record this as an observation about word order:** the pattern of the sentence does not change for either side; only the proportions change. **And on both sides the same cause yields the same effect: if there is a child, the share falls by half.**

### كَلَٰلَة — the analysis of the word

**This is the most technical and most disputed term in the sūra. It occurs in the twelfth verse, it occurs once more in the hundred and seventy-sixth, and the sūra closes with it.**

**The root ك-ل-ل.** The family of senses in the root:

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **كَلَّ** (*kalla*) | To grow weary, to have one's strength fail; to grow blunt (a knife ceasing to cut) |
| **كَلَال** (*kalāl*) | Weariness |
| **إِكْلِيل** (*iklīl*) | A crown — what **encircles** the head |
| **كُلّ** (*kull*) | All, the whole — that which encompasses |
| **تَكَلَّلَ** | To encompass, to surround |

**There are two clusters of sense: to grow weary/blunt, and to encompass.** And the lexicographers explain the term *kalāla* by both of them.

**The disagreement over what the term is:**

| View | Who or what *kalāla* is |
|---|---|
| **1** | **The deceased** — one who has neither child nor father |
| **2** | **The heirs** — relatives who come from the side, not bound to the deceased directly (as ascendants or descendants) |
| **3** | **The relationship itself** — the indirect, collateral tie |

**And the term's link with the language is explained as follows, which I relate as it is transmitted:** the estate arrives "wearily" — since it does not come along the direct line, it comes round about, encircling. Like the *iklīl* (the crown), it is arranged not at the centre but around **the edge**.

**It is plainly recorded in the sources that the definition of the word was disputed in the classical period, and it is transmitted that this disagreement has persisted from the earliest times.** **No preference is adopted in this section and no legal ruling is issued.**

**What follows from the wording of the verse and is not in dispute is this:** in the twelfth verse the share of the siblings named in the case of *kalāla* is a sixth and a third; in the hundred and seventy-sixth the share of the siblings named is a half, two-thirds, and *li'l-dhakari mithlu ḥaẓẓi'l-unthayayn*. **The proportions the two verses give are different**, and the explanation of that difference is a discussion in classical jurisprudence. **It is not entered into here.**

### شُرَكَآءُ فِى ٱلثُّلُثِ — partnership

**The root ش-ر-ك: partnership.** The word generally occurs in the Qurʾān in a negative context (*shirk*). **Here it is used in its technical and neutral sense: to be a partner in a share.**

**I record this as an observation about a word:** the root itself is not evil; what is evil is where the partnership is set up. The same record was entered in the treatment of `031-lokman.md` 31/13 as well.

### غَيْرَ مُضَآرٍّ — the limit of the bequest

**This was treated above (in the discussion of 4/11).** I record it once more here, because the qualification occurs only in this verse.

**And its position needs to be recorded: the qualification comes after the case of *kalāla*.** That is, in the case where the estate passes in the most indirect way, the possibility of the bequest turning into an instrument of dispossession is named expressly.

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the place of the qualification.

### وَصِيَّةً مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ — and the verse-ending

**The eleventh verse ended with *farīḍatan mina'llāh*; the twelfth ends with *waṣiyyatan mina'llāh*.**

**Both words point to the same place, but from different sides:** *farīḍa* — a notched measure; *waṣiyya* — a charge that has been attached. **And both with the same qualification: *mina'llāh*.**

**The verse-ending changes too:** the eleventh verse ends with *ʿAlīman Ḥakīmā*, the twelfth with ***ʿAlīmun Ḥalīm***.

**حَلِيم — root ح-ل-م: one who does not hurry, who is not carried away by his anger, who is grave.** The opposite of the word is *safah* (lightness), which occurred in the fifth verse.

**I record this as a weave of words:** the block opened in the fifth verse with the word *sufahāʾ* (the light ones); the twelfth verse closes with the name *Ḥalīm* (the grave one). **The two words are the two ends of the same measure, and both stand in the dictionary.**

**And the placing of the name *Ḥalīm* here must be recorded separately:** the division of an estate is one of the places where people lose their temper most quickly. **The verse sets the ruling beneath a name that does not hurry.** I give this as my own reading.

---

## 4/13-14 — تلك حدود الله ومن يطع الله ورسوله يدخله جنات تجري من تحتها الأنهار خالدين فيها وذلك الفوز العظيم · ومن يعص الله ورسوله ويتعد حدوده يدخله نارا خالدا فيها وله عذاب مهين

*Tilka ḥudūdu'llāh, wa man yuṭiʿi'llāha wa rasūlahū yudkhilhu jannātin tajrī min taḥtiha'l-anhāru khālidīna fīhā, wa dhālika'l-fawzu'l-ʿaẓīm · Wa man yaʿṣi'llāha wa rasūlahū wa yataʿadda ḥudūdahū yudkhilhu nāran khālidan fīhā wa lahū ʿadhābun muhīn*

"These are the limits set by God. Whoever obeys God and His Messenger, He will admit him into gardens beneath which rivers flow, to dwell there for ever. That is the great triumph. And whoever disobeys God and His Messenger and oversteps His limits, He will admit him into a fire, to dwell there for ever; and for him there is a humiliating punishment."

### The symmetry of the two verses

**The two verses are each other's reflection in a mirror, and this can be followed word by word:**

| | 13 | 14 |
|---|---|---|
| **Condition** | *Wa man yuṭiʿi* — the one who obeys | *Wa man yaʿṣi* — the one who disobeys |
| **Addition** | — | *Wa yataʿadda ḥudūdah* — and oversteps the limits |
| **Outcome** | *Yudkhilhu jannāt* | *Yudkhilhu nār* |
| **Duration** | *Khālidīna fīhā* (**plural**) | *Khālidan fīhā* (**singular**) |
| **Quality** | *Al-fawzu'l-ʿaẓīm* | *ʿAdhābun muhīn* |

**The difference in the fourth row is worth recording and is a datum of the text:** for the garden the **plural** is used (*khālidīn*), for the fire the **singular** (*khālidan*).

**Several explanations of this difference are given in the classical commentaries:** the flow of the sentence and the harmony of the verse-ending; or that the garden is shared while the fire is solitary. **I impose no preference;** the one certain thing is that the difference is there in the text.

### حُدُودُ ٱللَّهِ — and a word shared with al-Baqara

**The root ح-د-د: the place where a thing ends, an edge.** *Ḥadīd* — iron (a sharp edge); *ḥadd* — a limit; *ḥidād* — sharpness.

**The word occurred many times in the section on divorce in `002-bakara.md`, and it was recorded there that it is given in two different patterns:**

> *"**تِلْكَ حُدُودُ ٱللَّهِ فَلَا تَعْتَدُوهَا** — 'these are the limits set by God, do not overstep them.' The word **حُدُود** recurs many times in this section; in verse 187 it said *fa-lā taqrabūhā* (do not approach them), here *fa-lā taʿtadūhā* (do not overstep them). **Two patterns in two separate places: one of distance, one of a boundary.**"*

**I rely on that treatment.**

**And one observation must be recorded:** in al-Baqara the words *ḥudūdu'llāh* were placed at the end of the rulings on **divorce**; in al-Nisāʾ they are placed at the end of the rulings on **inheritance**. **In both places the word closes a block in which sharing within the family is regulated.** This is a similarity of structure between two sūras that can be verified.

### The character of the block's closing

**The verses of inheritance (11-12) are a wholly technical text: proportions, conditions, exceptions. The thirteenth and fourteenth verses set a frame over that technique.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the order of the verses:** having finished the reckoning, the sūra says **what the reckoning is**. The shares are not an arithmetic but a **limit**; and the overstepping of the limit is named not as an error in calculation but as an act of **disobedience**.

**And the choice of the verb *yataʿadda* must be recorded.** The root **ع-د-و**: to exceed the bound, to pass over onto something. **The verse does not say "the one who miscalculates"; it says "the one who oversteps the limit".** Injustice in the division of an estate is named not as a mistake in arithmetic but as a breach of a boundary.

### مُّهِين — the root ه-و-ن

**The root ه-و-ن: lightness, worthlessness, lowness.** *Hūn* — ease and lightness; *hawān* — abasement; *muhīn* — the one who abases.

**And the placing of the word here is worth recording:** what is done in a quarrel over an estate is to leave the other party out of the reckoning — that is, to count him as worth nothing. **The name of the recompense is given from the same root: *muhīn*.**

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the root of the word together with the subject of the block.

### What this says to the present

**One. That the bequest and the debt come first.** What is most often forgotten in the discussion of an estate is the qualification the verse repeats four times: **what is to be shared out is what remains after the obligations are taken out.** This is still today the point most often skipped — when the division of an estate is being discussed, the debts of the deceased usually come to mind last. The verse puts them first.

**Two. The rejection of the calculus of benefit.** The sentence *lā tadrūna ayyuhum aqrabu lakum nafʿā* closes the argument over "who deserved more". That argument is still where an inheritance does the most damage, and it usually runs on things that cannot be measured: who cared for him more, who was closer to him. **The verse ties the measure to what can be measured.**

**Three. And a limit.** How an actual estate is to be divided does not follow from here. **This section is not a text of *farāʾiḍ***; it analyses the wording and the terms of the verse. Application belongs to the law and the jurisprudence competent in it.

---

## 4/15-16 — واللاتي يأتين الفاحشة من نسائكم فاستشهدوا عليهن أربعة منكم فإن شهدوا فأمسكوهن في البيوت حتى يتوفاهن الموت أو يجعل الله لهن سبيلا · واللذان يأتيانها منكم فآذوهما فإن تابا وأصلحا فأعرضوا عنهما

*Wa'llātī yaʾtīna'l-fāḥishata min nisāʾikum fa'stashhidū ʿalayhinna arbaʿatan minkum, fa-in shahidū fa-amsikūhunna fi'l-buyūti ḥattā yatawaffāhunna'l-mawtu aw yajʿala'llāhu lahunna sabīlā · Wa'lladhāni yaʾtiyānihā minkum fa-ādhūhumā, fa-in tābā wa aṣlaḥā fa-aʿriḍū ʿanhumā, inna'llāha kāna Tawwāban Raḥīmā*

"Against those of your women who commit indecency, call four witnesses from among you. If they bear witness, keep them in the houses until death takes them or God appoints a way for them. As for the two among you who commit it, cause them hurt. If they repent and set themselves right, turn away from them. God is ever relenting, merciful."

### A note on method

**These two verses produced a long discussion in classical jurisprudence and exegesis, and that discussion is connected with Sūrat al-Nūr.**

**This field was treated in detail in the discussion of `024-nur.md` 24/2 and 24/4, and it was recorded plainly there:** *"This verse and those that follow it produced one of the widest discussions in classical jurisprudence. **In this section the disagreements within that discussion are transmitted, none of them is preferred and none of them is binding.** How the penalty is to be applied today, whether it is to be applied at all, and the current debates around this are not the subject of this text."*

**The same record holds here. I rely on that treatment and do not repeat what was treated there.** Only the points peculiar to these two verses will be treated here.

### أَرْبَعَةً مِّنكُمْ — four witnesses

**The condition of four witnesses was treated in detail in the discussion of `024-nur.md` 24/4 and 24/13.** The record made there was this, and it sits here: **by tying the accusation itself to four witnesses, it is in practice closed off.**

**And there is a detail in the word order here: *fa'stashhidū* — Form X.** The root **ش-ه-د**. Form X states **asking, demanding**: "demand that witnesses be produced."

**The verse, that is, places the accuser directly under an obligation.** The accusation gives rise to the demand for witnesses.

### أَوْ يَجْعَلَ ٱللَّهُ لَهُنَّ سَبِيلًا — the qualification left open

**The most striking side of the verse is that it carries a clause showing the ruling to be provisional in itself: "or until God appoints a way for them."**

**The explanation widely transmitted in the classical commentaries about this clause is the following, and I relate it as it is transmitted:** this is the announcement, in the verse's own wording, that the ruling is provisional; a "way" was opened by a later regulation.

**There are differing views in the classical literature as to which verse or which regulation this was. I impose no preference and issue no legal ruling.**

**What follows from the wording of the verse and is not in dispute is this:** the ruling carries within itself a **clause of termination**. This is not a structure often met with in the Qurʾān, and it is a datum of the text.

### فَـَٔاذُوهُمَا — the indefiniteness of the word

**The sixteenth verse provides for a measure against two persons but does not define the measure: *fa-ādhūhumā* — "cause them hurt".**

**The root أ-ذ-ي: to injure, to trouble.** The word was treated at `033-ahzab.md` 33/57-58; I rely on that treatment.

**And that the word is left without a measure must be recorded:** in Sūrat al-Nūr there was a number (*miʾata jalda*); here there is no number. **It is transmitted in the classical commentaries that this difference has to do with the ruling's being laid down by stages.**

**In this matter I impose no preference and issue no legal ruling.**

### فَإِن تَابَا وَأَصْلَحَا فَأَعْرِضُوا۟ عَنْهُمَا — the block's real qualification

**And this is the clause in the block that most needs to be dwelt on, because it comes immediately after the clause of the ruling.**

| Element | What it is |
|---|---|
| **تَابَا** | They repented — root **ت-و-ب**: to turn back |
| **أَصْلَحَا** | They set things right — root **ص-ل-ح** |
| **فَأَعْرِضُوا۟ عَنْهُمَا** | **Turn away, let it be** — root **ع-ر-ض** |

**That the three verbs work together must be recorded:** repentance alone is not enough, *iṣlāḥ* is asked for beside it; and in return the community is asked **to close the matter**.

**The expression فَأَعْرِضُوا۟ عَنْهُمَا does not say "forgive"; it says "turn away, do not press it".** What is commanded, that is, is that the matter not be kept on the agenda.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the verb itself.** And it looks in the same direction as the measure treated throughout `024-nur.md`: **as the sūra closes a matter, so it closes the talking about it.**

### The root ص-ل-ح and its place in the sūra

**This root is one of the busiest in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ, and its places can be counted:**

| Verse | Word | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **16** | *aṣlaḥā* | The close of the discussion of indecency |
| **35** | *iṣlāḥan*, *yuwaffiqi'llāhu baynahumā* | The close of the discussion of conflict within the family |
| **114** | *iṣlāḥin bayna'l-nās* | The good kind of secret conversation |
| **128** | *an yuṣliḥā baynahumā ṣulḥā*, *wa'l-ṣulḥu khayr* | Between husband and wife |
| **129** | *wa in tuṣliḥū wa tattaqū* | The close of the discussion of justice |

**Four of the five places are where a dispute comes to an end.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the sūra tends to close its rulings not with a sanction but with **setting right**. This will be shown separately in the discussion of the thirty-fifth verse.

---

## 4/17-18 — إنما التوبة على الله للذين يعملون السوء بجهالة ثم يتوبون من قريب فأولئك يتوب الله عليهم · وليست التوبة للذين يعملون السيئات حتى إذا حضر أحدهم الموت قال إني تبت الآن

*Innama'l-tawbatu ʿala'llāhi li'lladhīna yaʿmalūna'l-sūʾa bi-jahālatin thumma yatūbūna min qarībin fa-ulāʾika yatūbu'llāhu ʿalayhim, wa kāna'llāhu ʿAlīman Ḥakīmā · Wa laysati'l-tawbatu li'lladhīna yaʿmalūna'l-sayyiʾāti ḥattā idhā ḥaḍara aḥadahumu'l-mawtu qāla innī tubtu'l-āna wa la'lladhīna yamūtūna wa hum kuffār, ulāʾika aʿtadnā lahum ʿadhāban alīmā*

"The repentance God will accept is only that of those who do evil in ignorance and then repent soon after. It is these whose repentance God accepts. But there is no repentance for those who go on doing evil deeds until, when death comes to one of them, he says 'now I repent'; nor for those who die as deniers. For these We have prepared a painful punishment."

### The opposition of the two verses

**The seventeenth verse describes the case in which repentance is accepted, the eighteenth the case in which it is not. The two form a pair.**

| | 17 | 18 |
|---|---|---|
| **Verb** | *Yaʿmalūna'l-sūʾ* — singular, a generic noun | *Yaʿmalūna'l-sayyiʾāt* — **plural** |
| **Qualification** | *Bi-jahāla* — in ignorance | No qualification |
| **Time** | *Min qarīb* — soon, within a short time | *Ḥattā idhā ḥaḍara aḥadahumu'l-mawt* — until death comes |
| **Speech** | — | *Innī tubtu'l-āna* — "now I repent" |

**In every one of the four rows there is an opposition, and this is read off the words themselves.**

### بِجَهَٰلَة — the sense of the word

**The root ج-ه-ل. And the distinction the lexicographers record must be noted:** *jahl* is not only "ignorance"; it also carries the sense of **feeling overpowering the mind, of excess, of acting without thinking**. The word *jāhiliyya* too is explained by this second sense.

**This is needed if the verse is to be understood.** If *jahāla* were only "not knowing", the door of repentance would be closed to one who sins knowingly — and that does not fit with other verses of the Qurʾān (al-Zumar 39/53; treated in `039-zumer.md`).

**Two tendencies are transmitted in the classical commentaries about this word:**

| View | What *jahāla* is |
|---|---|
| **1** | Not knowing the ruling, not being aware |
| **2** | To act **without reckoning the consequence, in a moment of excess** — even while knowing |

**The second view is widely transmitted in the classical commentaries and is in keeping with the family of senses in the root. I impose no preference.**

### مِن قَرِيبٍ — the interval

**"Soon" — no length of time is specified, a nearness is specified.**

**And the eighteenth verse shows the limit of that nearness: the coming of death.** The measure, that is, is not a calendar but a **possibility**: repentance is what is done while something can still be done.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the opposition of the two verses.**

### إِنِّى تُبْتُ ٱلْـَٰٔنَ — the words themselves

**The verse relates the repentance that is rejected as a sentence: "now I repent."**

**The word inside the sentence must be recorded: *al-āna* — "now".**

**I record this as an observation about word order:** what is rejected is not the content of the repentance but its **timing**. And the word that states the timing is set inside the sentence. That is, in relating the words the verse also shows the flaw in the words within the same sentence.

### What cannot be drawn from this discussion

**No conclusion such as "after a certain sin the door of repentance is closed" follows from these two verses**, and this must be written plainly.

**The grounds:**

**One.** The verb of the seventeenth verse is *yaʿmalūna'l-sūʾ* — a generic noun, and it specifies no kind of sin.
**Two.** What is rejected in the eighteenth verse is repentance **postponed to the moment of death**; no limit is set on repentance made early.
**Three.** Elsewhere the Qurʾān holds the door of repentance widely open (al-Zumar 39/53). **No contradiction can be set up between the two verses.**

**And one further record: no judgement about any person can be drawn from these verses.** Which category a person's repentance falls into lies outside human knowledge. **The function of the verse is not an instrument of diagnosis but a warning about timing.**

---

## 4/19 — يا أيها الذين آمنوا لا يحل لكم أن ترثوا النساء كرها ولا تعضلوهن لتذهبوا ببعض ما آتيتموهن إلا أن يأتين بفاحشة مبينة وعاشروهن بالمعروف فإن كرهتموهن فعسى أن تكرهوا شيئا ويجعل الله فيه خيرا كثيرا

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū lā yaḥillu lakum an tarithu'l-nisāʾa karhā, wa lā taʿḍulūhunna li-tadhhabū bi-baʿḍi mā ātaytumūhunna illā an yaʾtīna bi-fāḥishatin mubayyina, wa ʿāshirūhunna bi'l-maʿrūf, fa-in karihtumūhunna fa-ʿasā an takrahū shayʾan wa yajʿala'llāhu fīhi khayran kathīrā*

"O you who believe! It is not lawful for you to inherit women against their will. And do not press them in order to take back part of what you have given them — unless they commit a manifest indecency. And live with them in kindness. If you dislike them — it may be that you dislike a thing in which God has placed much good."

### The three commands of the verse

**The verse says three things, and the three come one after another:**

| Order | Clause | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *Lā yaḥillu lakum an tarithu'l-nisāʾa karhā* | **A prohibition** — women cannot be made an object of inheritance |
| **2** | *Wa lā taʿḍulūhunna li-tadhhabū bi-baʿḍi mā ātaytumūhunna* | **A prohibition** — they cannot be pressed in order to take back what was given |
| **3** | *Wa ʿāshirūhunna bi'l-maʿrūf* | **A command** — live with them in kindness |

**Two prohibitions and one command.** And the command comes after the two prohibitions: **first what is not to be done, then what is.**

### أَن تَرِثُوا۟ ٱلنِّسَآءَ كَرْهًا — one of the sharpest sentences in the sūra

The seventh verse said that women take a share **of** an estate. The nineteenth verse says that women *cannot be taken* **as** an estate.

**Twelve verses stand between the two, and both use the same root: و-ر-ث.**

| Verse | Word | Where the woman stands |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | *naṣībun mimmā taraka* | **An heir** — the party who takes a share |
| **19** | *an tarithu'l-nisāʾa* | **Cannot be an object of inheritance** — not the thing taken |

**This is a weave of roots in the sūra that can be verified, and I record it as my own reading:** the same root, twelve verses apart, establishes two separate sides of one right.

**And the word *karhan* must be recorded. The root ك-ر-ه:** to dislike, not to want; and *karh* — compulsion, unwillingness. The word was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/216 (*wa huwa kurhun lakum*).

**There are two readings of the word's case, and both are found in classical exegesis:**

| Reading | To whom *karhan* belongs |
|---|---|
| **1** | **To the women** — against their will |
| **2** | **General** — the transaction itself rests on compulsion |

**I impose no preference;** both come out at the same place.

### Historical background

**It is transmitted in the sources that in that period, when a man died, the wife he left behind was regarded by the deceased's relatives as part of the estate:** she could be married, given to another, or prevented from marrying so that her property might be kept in hand.

**I relate this in the language of "it is transmitted"; I give no name of a person or a tribe.** What the wording of the verse says is clear in any case: the verb is *tarithū* (to inherit) and its object is *al-nisāʾ*.

### وَلَا تَعْضُلُوهُنَّ — the root ع-ض-ل

**This root was analysed at `002-bakara.md` 2/232, and it was recorded there:**

> *"**فَلَا تَعْضُلُوهُنَّ** — root **ع-ض-ل**. The concrete sense the lexicographers record is striking: *aʿḍalati'l-nāqa* — **the blocking of a camel's delivery, the young unable to come out.** From there comes the sense of 'to press, to block someone's way, to leave without a way out'. **The choice of the word shows the character of the conduct that is forbidden:** the obstruction is set up not by a ruling but **by the blocking of the way out**."*

**I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it here.**

**Comparing the two verses is worth recording:**

| Place | What *ʿaḍl* serves | Its purpose |
|---|---|---|
| al-Baqara 2/232 | To prevent a divorced woman from **marrying again** | — |
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/19** | To **press and hold** a woman within the marriage | *Li-tadhhabū bi-baʿḍi mā ātaytumūhunna* — to take back part of what was given |

**In the use in al-Nisāʾ the purpose is written out openly:** a financial gain. **In al-Baqara the purpose was not written.** This is a verifiable difference between the two verses.

### إِلَّآ أَن يَأْتِينَ بِفَٰحِشَةٍ مُّبَيِّنَةٍ — the scope of the exception

**There is a classical disagreement over what the exception attaches to and over what a *fāḥisha mubayyina* is. The transmitted views are given in a table; no preference is imposed and no legal ruling is issued.**

| View | What the exception refers back to |
|---|---|
| **1** | To the prohibition of *ʿaḍl* — in a particular situation a woman's separating for a payment may come into question |
| **2** | To the prohibition of inheriting |
| **3** | To the sentence as a whole |

**And the word *mubayyina* must be recorded:** root **ب-ي-ن**, active participle — **"that makes itself plain"**. That is, not an imputation resting on suspicion but a situation whose plainness comes from itself.

**This record matters because the same field was treated at `024-nur.md` 24/4: the accusation itself was tied to four witnesses.** Read together, the two verses show that the exception is not a door that can be worked easily.

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the pattern of the word together with the verse of al-Nūr.

### وَعَاشِرُوهُنَّ بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ — the centre of the verse

**عَاشِرُوا۟ — root ع-ش-ر, Form III (*mufāʿala*).** And the pattern carries information.

**The concrete sense of the root: *ʿashara* — ten; *ʿashīra* — a tribe, a community living together; *muʿāshara* — to live together, to be intertwined.**

**The function of Form III must be recorded: reciprocity.** Verbs in this pattern state acts performed by two parties together (*qātala* — to fight, *shāraka* — to be a partner, *sābaqa* — to race).

**So *ʿāshirūhunna* does not mean "treat them well" but "live together with them, each with the other".** The verb states not a one-way treatment but **a shared life**.

I record this as a **grammatical observation**; its ground is the form itself.

**And the qualification *bi'l-maʿrūf*:** root **ع-ر-ف** — known, recognised. The measure is the measure known in that community. **The word draws the limit of a ruling in six places in this sūra (5, 6, 8, 19, 25, 114).**

### فَإِن كَرِهْتُمُوهُنَّ فَعَسَىٰٓ أَن تَكْرَهُوا۟ شَيْـًٔا وَيَجْعَلَ ٱللَّهُ فِيهِ خَيْرًا كَثِيرًا

**And the last sentence of the verse is the very same pattern as one in al-Baqara.**

**This pattern was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/216, and what was recorded there sits directly here:**

> *"The most striking side of the verse is that in laying down the ruling it **does not deny the aversion**… **The ruling is laid down without disregarding what the one addressed feels.**
> **عَسَىٰٓ** — 'it may be'. This particle states no certainty. **The verse, that is, does not say 'everything you dislike is good';** it says that the link between the two is **not necessary**."*

**I rely on that treatment without fail.** And I compare the two verses:

| | **al-Baqara 2/216** | **al-Nisāʾ 4/19** |
|---|---|---|
| **Subject** | War (*al-qitāl*) | Disliking one's wife |
| **The acknowledgement of aversion** | *Wa huwa kurhun lakum* | *Fa-in karihtumūhunna* |
| **Particle** | *ʿAsā* | *ʿAsā* |
| **Structure** | Two-directional: what you love may also be an ill | **One-directional**: good may lie only in what you dislike |
| **Closing** | *Wa'llāhu yaʿlamu wa antum lā taʿlamūn* | *Wa yajʿala'llāhu fīhi khayran kathīrā* |

**The difference in the fourth row is a datum of the text and must be recorded:** the sentence in al-Baqara was built in two directions (what you dislike may be good, what you love may be an ill); **the sentence in al-Nisāʾ states only the first direction.**

**I record this as my own reading and propose a ground for it:** in al-Baqara the one addressed was being called to an obligation, and a warning in both directions was needed. **In al-Nisāʾ the one addressed is on the threshold of ending a relationship; the sentence is trying to delay that decision.** This is an interpretation, not a transmitted report.

**And what the sentence does not say must also be recorded:**

**The verse does not say "endure it even though you dislike it".** The particle *ʿasā* states no certainty, and the verse guarantees no outcome. What is said is this: **aversion is not, by itself, a datum for a ruling.** What a person feels at a given moment does not carry sufficient information about the whole of a relationship.

**And it cannot be concluded from this sentence that a party who is being harmed must keep the situation going.** The sūra itself regulates separation (the section on divorce in `002-bakara.md`, and al-Nisāʾ 4/35, 4/128, 4/130), and the hundred and thirtieth verse says plainly: *wa in yatafarraqā yughni'llāhu kullan min saʿatih* — **"if they part, God will enrich each of them out of His abundance."**

**The sūra, that is, encourages staying together without closing the door to separation.** This is a balance within the sūra's own text, and it can be verified by the presence of the two verses.

---

## 4/20-21 — وإن أردتم استبدال زوج مكان زوج وآتيتم إحداهن قنطارا فلا تأخذوا منه شيئا أتأخذونه بهتانا وإثما مبينا · وكيف تأخذونه وقد أفضى بعضكم إلى بعض وأخذن منكم ميثاقا غليظا

*Wa in aradtumu'stibdāla zawjin makāna zawjin wa ātaytum iḥdāhunna qinṭāran fa-lā taʾkhudhū minhu shayʾā, a-taʾkhudhūnahū buhtānan wa ithman mubīnā · Wa kayfa taʾkhudhūnahū wa qad afḍā baʿḍukum ilā baʿḍin wa akhadhna minkum mīthāqan ghalīẓā*

"If you wish to take one wife in the place of another, and you have given one of them a heap of wealth, take nothing back from it. Would you take it by slander and manifest sin? And how could you take it, when you have gone in to one another and they have taken from you a solemn pledge?"

### قِنطَار — the measure of the word

**قِنطَار — root ق-ن-ط-ر.** The lexicographers differ over whether the word states a definite measure of weight or "much wealth" in general; **I cannot give a definite amount and do not give one.**

**From the same root comes *qanṭara* — a bridge.** The link the lexicographers make is this: a bridge is the structure that **binds** two banks together; and a *qinṭār* is wealth gathered and bound. **I relate this link as the lexicographers record it.**

**And the function of the word here must be recorded:** by carrying the amount to its furthest extreme, the verse tests the ruling. **"Even if you have given a heap of wealth" — that is, whatever the amount, the ruling does not change.** This is a technique of argument (*iḥtijāj*) in law: the rule is reinforced by giving the extreme case.

### أَتَأْخُذُونَهُۥ بُهْتَٰنًا — the form of the question

**Having laid down the prohibition, the verse asks a question: "would you take it by slander?"**

**And in Arabic this is an *istifhām inkārī*: a question that expects no answer and states a rejection.** The pattern was treated in many places in this commentary.

**بُهْتَٰن — root ب-ه-ت: to astound, to leave someone stunned.** *Buhita* — he was left dumbfounded. And *buhtān* is an imputation so baseless as to **leave the other party stunned**. **The word was treated at `024-nur.md` 24/16 and at `033-ahzab.md` 33/58; I rely on those treatments.**

**What was recorded in the discussion of al-Aḥzāb was this: *buhtān* names the offence by the state of the victim** — that is, the word itself carries the effect the imputation leaves on the other party.

**And the context here must be recorded:** to invent an accusation in order to take back property. **The verse describes a financial greed producing a slander** and joins the two acts in one sentence.

### وَقَدْ أَفْضَىٰ بَعْضُكُمْ إِلَىٰ بَعْضٍ — the analysis of the word

**أَفْضَىٰ — root ف-ض-و.** And the concrete sense of the root must be recorded: **فَضَاء (*faḍāʾ*) — emptiness, openness, wide space.** *Arḍun faḍāʾ* — open ground without obstruction.

**And the verb is in Form IV: *afḍā ilā* — to open out towards something, to reach it with no barrier left in between.**

**The picture in the word, then, is this: the lifting of the veil between two things, their opening to one another.**

**The lexicographers explain the word here as "to mingle with one another, to come together in intimacy"**, and in the classical commentaries it is said that this points to the intimacy of married life.

**And the placing of the word here is the finest point of the verse. I record this as my own reading:** in refusing a financial claim, the verse **gives no financial ground.** It does not say "that property is hers" or "you have no legal right". **The ground it gives is that the veil between them has been lifted.**

**A legal text, that is, rests a rule of law on intimacy.**

### مِّيثَٰقًا غَلِيظًا — and a link within the sūra

**The root م-ث-ق (و-ث-ق): to bind firmly.** *Wathīqa* — a document; *thiqa* — trustworthy; *mīthāq* — a word bound fast.

**غَلِيظ — root غ-ل-ظ: thick, coarse, heavy.** *Ghilẓa* — thickness.

**And now comes a datum of the text, and it can be verified within the sūra:**

**This exact expression — *mīthāqan ghalīẓā* — occurs in three places in the Qurʾān:**

| Place | Whose pledge |
|---|---|
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/21** | **The marriage bond between husband and wife** |
| al-Nisāʾ 4/154 | A pledge taken from a community |
| al-Aḥzāb 33/7 | **The pledge taken from the prophets** |

**That verse was treated at `033-ahzab.md` 33/7; I rely on that treatment.**

**Comparing these three places yields an observation, which I record as my own reading:** the expression the Qurʾān uses for the pledge taken from the prophets **is used for marriage as well.** No equality is set up between the two bonds; but the word that states the **weight** of the pledge is the same.

**And there is one further detail of the word order:** the verse does not say *wa akhadhtum minhunna* (you took from them); it says ***wa akhadhna minkum*** (they took from you). **The party who took the pledge is the women.**

**This is the wording of the sentence and a datum of the text.** I draw no ruling from it; I record the subject of the verb.

### What this says to the present

**One.** The three verses between the nineteenth and the twenty-first take aim at a single pattern of conduct: **taking back, at the end of a relationship, what is in the other party's hands.** The verse closes this off three times, by three separate routes — holding by force, pressing, and slander.

**Two.** That the command *ʿāshirūhunna bi'l-maʿrūf* comes in Form III shows that the relationship is named not as a one-way treatment but as **a shared life**. This is something that can be read off the grammar.

**Three.** The ground given by the twenty-first verse is as valid today as it was then: **where a relationship ends, the intimacy that relationship built cannot be turned into a bargaining counter.** The verse says this with the verb *afḍā*.

**And a limit:** it does not follow from these verses that one party is right in every case and the other wrong in every case. The verse forbids **a particular act** and lays down its exception itself. **No sweeping ruling is made about a group.**

---

## 4/22 — ولا تنكحوا ما نكح آباؤكم من النساء إلا ما قد سلف إنه كان فاحشة ومقتا وساء سبيلا

*Wa lā tankiḥū mā nakaḥa ābāʾukum mina'l-nisāʾi illā mā qad salaf, innahū kāna fāḥishatan wa maqtan wa sāʾa sabīlā*

"Do not marry the women your fathers married — except what is already past. That is an indecency, a thing loathed, and an evil way."

### The position of the verse

**This verse is the continuation of the prohibition in the nineteenth.** There it was said that women cannot be taken as an estate; here the most concrete form of that practice is named.

**And there is a relation between it and the twenty-third verse:** the twenty-third will give a whole list; this verse stands **before** the list and **on its own**.

**I record this as an observation about word order:** its being named separately shows that the ruling is newly laid down and meets a practice of the ones addressed. **The list will come in the twenty-third verse; this verse abolishes a practice.**

### Three descriptions

**The verse gives the prohibition three names, and the three come from different directions:**

| Word | Root | What it looks to |
|---|---|---|
| **فَٰحِشَة** | **ف-ح-ش** — an ugliness exceeding all measure | **The act itself** |
| **مَقْت** | **م-ق-ت** — violent loathing, revulsion | **The feeling it arouses** |
| **سَآءَ سَبِيلًا** | **س-و-أ** — to be evil | **Where it leads** |

**Three words name the act from three separate sides: what it is, what it makes one feel, where it leads.**

**The root م-ق-ت is worth recording:** the lexicographers explain the word as "the most violent hatred". The word is rare in the Qurʾān and occurs at `040-gafir.md` 40/10 and 40/35; I rely on those treatments.

**And there is a linguistic datum recorded in the classical sources, which I relate as it is transmitted:** it is said that in pre-Islamic Arab society the term ***nikāḥ al-maqt*** ("the marriage of loathing") was used for a marriage of this kind. **That the verse uses the same word is for this reason worth noticing**; but I give this not as certain historical information but as a record transmitted in the sources.

### إِلَّا مَا قَدْ سَلَفَ — the leaving of the past outside

**The root س-ل-ف: to go before, to be left behind.** *Salaf* — those who went before; *sālif* — past.

**The function of the clause must be recorded:** the ruling is laid down looking forward, and no sanction is provided for the past. **The same qualification will come once more at the end of the twenty-third verse.**

**I record this as my own reading:** that where a new ruling is laid down, the life lived before the ruling is not judged by it, is a recurring qualification in this sūra.

---

## 4/23 — حرمت عليكم أمهاتكم وبناتكم وأخواتكم وعماتكم وخالاتكم وبنات الأخ وبنات الأخت وأمهاتكم اللاتي أرضعنكم وأخواتكم من الرضاعة وأمهات نسائكم وربائبكم اللاتي في حجوركم … وأن تجمعوا بين الأختين إلا ما قد سلف

*Ḥurrimat ʿalaykum ummahātukum wa banātukum wa akhawātukum wa ʿammātukum wa khālātukum wa banātu'l-akhi wa banātu'l-ukhti wa ummahātukumu'llātī arḍaʿnakum wa akhawātukum mina'l-raḍāʿati wa ummahātu nisāʾikum wa rabāʾibukumu'llātī fī ḥujūrikum min nisāʾikumu'llātī dakhaltum bihinna, fa-in lam takūnū dakhaltum bihinna fa-lā junāḥa ʿalaykum, wa ḥalāʾilu abnāʾikumu'lladhīna min aṣlābikum wa an tajmaʿū bayna'l-ukhtayni illā mā qad salaf, inna'llāha kāna Ghafūran Raḥīmā*

"Forbidden to you are: your mothers, your daughters, your sisters, your paternal aunts, your maternal aunts, your brother's daughters, your sister's daughters, the mothers who nursed you, your sisters through nursing, the mothers of your wives, your stepdaughters in your care, born of wives you have gone in to — if you have not gone in to them there is no fault upon you — the wives of your sons who are of your own loins, and that you should join two sisters together. Except what is already past. God is forgiving, merciful."

### A note on method

**This verse is the foundational text of the discussion of marriage in classical jurisprudence, and detailed rulings have been developed upon every item in it.**

**What is done in this section is this: the verse's list is set in order, the terms are analysed, and the logic of the list is shown.** On such matters as the scope of the tie of nursing, the position of the stepdaughter, and whether the qualification "in your care" is a condition, **there are differences among the schools of law; those differences are not entered into in this section, no preference is adopted and no legal ruling is issued.**

### The order of the list

**The verse counts thirteen items, and these fall into four groups. This is the verse's own order:**

| Group | Items | The kind of tie |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Descent** | Mothers, daughters, sisters, paternal aunts, maternal aunts, brothers' daughters, sisters' daughters | **The tie of blood** |
| **2. Nursing** | The mothers who nursed, sisters through nursing | **The tie of milk** |
| **3. Affinity** | The mothers of wives, stepdaughters, sons' wives | **The tie made by marriage** |
| **4. The prohibition of joining** | Marrying two sisters at once | **The overlapping of ties** |

**And the order itself must be recorded:** the list begins with the tie of blood, continues with the tie of milk, and ends with the tie made by marriage. **It moves, that is, from the most natural to the most constructed.**

**I record this as my own reading; its ground is the verse's own order.**

### رَضَاعَة — the tie of milk

**The root ر-ض-ع: to suckle.** And this item of the verse is worth recording: **a tie produces the same effect as a blood tie although it is not one.**

**I record this as an observation:** the verse does not tie the source of a bond to descent alone. That a child grew up on a woman's milk establishes a legal tie.

**On the scope of the tie of nursing, how many feedings establish it and up to what age it holds, there is a wide disagreement in classical jurisprudence. That disagreement is not entered into in this section and no ruling is issued.**

### رَبَٰٓئِب — stepdaughters

**The root ر-ب-ب — the same root as *rabb* in the first verse.** *Rabība*: a girl who grows up **under a person's nurture**. The word itself contains the relation of care.

**And the qualification in the verse must be recorded: *allātī fī ḥujūrikum* — "who are in your laps / in your houses".**

**حِجْر — root ح-ج-ر: the lap; and also a barrier, a bound (*ḥijr* — a forbidden zone; *ḥajr* — legal interdiction).** The two senses of the word — lap and bound — are in the same root.

**Whether this qualification is a condition or a description of the usual case is one of the disputed questions of classical jurisprudence. I impose no preference and issue no ruling.**

**But the choice of the word yields an observation, which I record as my own reading:** the word *ḥujūr* carries both "lap" and "bound". The word holds within itself the link between protection and prohibition.

### ٱلَّذِينَ مِنْ أَصْلَٰبِكُمْ — the function of the qualification

**In counting the wives of sons, a qualification is added: *alladhīna min aṣlābikum* — "who are of your own loins".**

**This qualification is directly connected with the matter treated at `033-ahzab.md` 33/4-5 and 33/37:** that an adopted child is not counted as a child of one's own.

**The principle recorded in the discussion of al-Aḥzāb was this:** *"calling a child 'my son' does not make him one's own son."* **This qualification in al-Nisāʾ is the counterpart of the same principle within a ruling on marriage. I rely on that treatment.**

**This link between the two sūras is a datum of the text, and the places of the words can be counted.**

### The logic of the list — an observation

**What do those counted in the list have in common?**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the list itself:** all the ties counted are ties **the person did not choose** or that are **already established**. Mother, daughter, sister, paternal aunt, maternal aunt — these are not chosen. The tie of milk — it is formed in childhood. Mother-in-law, stepdaughter, daughter-in-law — these arise from a marriage already made.

**The list, that is, prevents a new tie from being built on top of existing ties.** And the prohibition of marrying two sisters at once is the clearest instance of this: **a tie already exists between two sisters, and a marriage could turn that tie into a rivalry.**

**This is an interpretation, not a transmitted report.** But it can be read from the list itself.

### إِلَّا مَا قَدْ سَلَفَ — a second time

**It occurred in the twenty-second verse too. It is repeated two verses later.**

**And the verse-ending changes:** the twenty-second verse ended with *sāʾa sabīlā* (an evil way); the twenty-third ends with ***Ghafūran Raḥīmā***.

**I record this as an observation about word order:** the same exception occurs twice, but one closes with reproach and the other with forgiveness. **That the new ruling does not run backwards is said with two different names.**

---

## 4/24 — والمحصنات من النساء إلا ما ملكت أيمانكم كتاب الله عليكم وأحل لكم ما وراء ذلكم أن تبتغوا بأموالكم محصنين غير مسافحين

*Wa'l-muḥṣanātu mina'l-nisāʾi illā mā malakat aymānukum, kitāba'llāhi ʿalaykum, wa uḥilla lakum mā warāʾa dhālikum an tabtaghū bi-amwālikum muḥṣinīna ghayra musāfiḥīn, fa-ma'stamtaʿtum bihī minhunna fa-ātūhunna ujūrahunna farīḍatan, wa lā junāḥa ʿalaykum fīmā tarāḍaytum bihī min baʿdi'l-farīḍa, inna'llāha kāna ʿAlīman Ḥakīmā*

"And married women are forbidden to you — except those your right hands possess. This is God's writ upon you. Apart from these, it is made lawful for you to seek with your wealth, in chastity and not in fornication. To those of them from whom you have had enjoyment, give their bridal gifts as an appointed due. There is no fault upon you in what you agree between you after the appointed due. God is Knowing, Wise."

### مُحْصَنَات — the analysis of the word

**The root ح-ص-ن: to be protected, to be made secure.** *Ḥiṣn* — a fortress; *ḥaṣīn* — firm; *muḥṣan* — protected, brought under protection.

**The word was treated in the discussion of `024-nur.md` 24/4, and it was recorded there that there is disagreement over its scope. I rely on that treatment.**

**There is a classical disagreement over the sense in which the word comes in this verse:**

| View | Who the *muḥṣanāt* are |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Married women** — those brought under protection by marriage |
| **2** | **Chaste women** — in general |
| **3** | **Free women** |

**This disagreement is real in the classical commentaries. The context (a list of marriage prohibitions) supports the first reading; I impose no preference.**

**And an interesting linguistic datum must be recorded:** in the continuation of the same verse the word is used this time **of men**: *muḥṣinīna ghayra musāfiḥīn* — "in chastity, not in fornication". **The root, that is, is applied to both sides.**

**This is a countable fact, and it is within a single verse.**

### إِلَّا مَا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَٰنُكُمْ — a record

**This expression occurs several times in the sūra (3, 24, 25, 36, 92) and concerns an institution of society that existed in the classical period.**

**A record was entered on this matter in the discussion of `033-ahzab.md` 33/50, and I carry it over here as it stands:**

> *"**No legal ruling was issued on the expression *mā malakat aymānuhum* in 33/50.** The Qurʾān's other records on the same subject (the bringing forward of manumission as a good deed and as an expiation) were recorded **without concealment**; it was stated that the matter was discussed at length in the history of Islamic thought and that it **exceeds the bounds of this commentary**."*

**The same record holds here.**

**And the Qurʾān's own data can be recorded:** in the ninety-second verse of this sūra **the freeing of a neck** (*taḥrīru raqabatin muʾmina*) is provided as the expiation for killing by mistake; in the thirty-sixth verse *mā malakat aymānukum* is included in the list of kindness; and in the twenty-fifth verse the manner of marriage with women in this position is regulated and **their bridal gifts are given to them**.

**These are data of the text. I build no thesis about history from them and draw no ruling.**

### كِتَٰبَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ — the function of the clause

**"God's writ upon you" — an emphasis in the pattern of the absolute object (*mafʿūl muṭlaq*).**

**The sense "to write an obligation" in the root ك-ت-ب was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/2:** *"kutiba ʿalaykum — 'it was written for you', that is, it was made obligatory… In this usage a book is not only what is read but a text that **brings an obligation**."* **I rely on that treatment.**

### فَمَا ٱسْتَمْتَعْتُم بِهِۦ مِنْهُنَّ — a disputed expression

**This expression has been the subject of different readings in the history of Islamic thought, and this is a known disagreement.**

**What is done in this section is this: the existence of the disagreement is recorded, no choice is made between the parties, no insinuation is made against any group, and no polemic is entered into.**

| Reading | What *istimtāʿ* is |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Enjoyment of the marriage itself** — the expression describes ordinary marriage, and the verse regulates the bridal gift. **This is the reading adopted by the great majority of the classical commentaries** |
| **2** | The view that the expression points to a particular practice, and that its ruling is disputed |

**This divergence is a question of jurisprudence whose outcome differs among the schools of law. I make no choice, I issue no legal ruling, and none of them is binding.**

**What follows from the wording of the verse and is not in dispute is this:** the command of the sentence is the payment of the bridal gift (*fa-ātūhunna ujūrahunna farīḍatan*), and this command is a repetition of the command in the sūra's fourth verse (*wa ātu'l-nisāʾa ṣaduqātihinna*).

### أُجُور — a third word

**The sūra uses a third word for the bridal gift: *ajr*.**

| Verse | Word | The root's stress |
|---|---|---|
| 4 | *ṣaduqāt* | **ص-د-ق** — truthfulness |
| 24, 25 | *ujūr* | **أ-ج-ر** — return, wage |
| 24 | *farīḍa* | **ف-ر-ض** — a notched measure |

**Three words name the same legal element from three separate sides.** This is a countable fact, and it was recorded in the discussion of the fourth verse as well.

### وَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ فِيمَا تَرَٰضَيْتُم بِهِۦ — the field of consent

**تَرَٰضَيْتُم — root ر-ض-و, Form VI: mutual consent.**

**The same pattern was treated at `002-bakara.md` 2/233** (*ʿan tarāḍin minhumā wa tashāwur*) and it was recorded there: *"Both words are from Forms III and VI, that is, from the patterns that state reciprocity."* **I rely on that treatment.**

**And the place of the qualification must be recorded:** the verse first lays down a measure (*farīḍa*), then leaves room for mutual agreement **above** that measure. **The law, that is, draws a floor and leaves the ceiling to the parties.**

I record this as **my own reading**; its ground is the qualification *min baʿdi'l-farīḍa*.

---

## 4/25 — ومن لم يستطع منكم طولا أن ينكح المحصنات المؤمنات فمن ما ملكت أيمانكم من فتياتكم المؤمنات … فانكحوهن بإذن أهلهن وآتوهن أجورهن بالمعروف … ذلك لمن خشي العنت منكم وأن تصبروا خير لكم

*Wa man lam yastaṭiʿ minkum ṭawlan an yankiḥa'l-muḥṣanāti'l-muʾmināti fa-min mā malakat aymānukum min fatayātikumu'l-muʾmināt, wa'llāhu aʿlamu bi-īmānikum, baʿḍukum min baʿḍ, fa'nkiḥūhunna bi-idhni ahlihinna wa ātūhunna ujūrahunna bi'l-maʿrūfi muḥṣanātin ghayra musāfiḥātin wa lā muttakhidhāti akhdān… dhālika li-man khashiya'l-ʿanata minkum, wa an taṣbirū khayrun lakum, wa'llāhu Ghafūrun Raḥīm*

"Whoever among you cannot afford to marry free believing women, let him marry from among the believing young women your right hands possess. God knows your faith best. **You are all of one another.** Marry them with the permission of their people and give them their bridal gifts in the recognised manner… This is for those among you who fear falling into hardship. That you be patient is better for you. God is forgiving, merciful."

### بَعْضُكُم مِّنۢ بَعْضٍ — the clause in the middle of the verse

**Into the middle of a long clause of ruling a sentence of four words has been set: *baʿḍukum min baʿḍ* — "you are, one of you, from another."**

**And this sentence ties back to the sūra's first verse:** *khalaqakum min nafsin wāḥida*.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the shared logic of the two verses:** the first verse said that humankind comes from a single root. The twenty-fifth verse, while giving a ruling about the section of society seen as lowest, **says the same thing again.**

**The sentence, that is, is a reminder set into the middle of a ruling: the social difference between the parties is not a difference in respect of being human.**

### وَٱللَّهُ أَعْلَمُ بِإِيمَٰنِكُمْ — a second parenthetical clause

**And immediately before it there is one more sentence: "God knows your faith best."**

**About the function of this sentence the following is widely transmitted in the classical commentaries:** it is a qualification set in the path of people's ranking one another by faith.

**I relate this explanation as it is transmitted.** And the order of the verse supports it: the sentence comes immediately after the expression "believing young women".

### بِإِذْنِ أَهْلِهِنَّ وَءَاتُوهُنَّ أُجُورَهُنَّ — two qualifications

**The verse lays down two conditions:**

| Qualification | What it is |
|---|---|
| *Bi-idhni ahlihinna* | The permission of their people |
| *Wa ātūhunna ujūrahunna bi'l-maʿrūf* | **That their bridal gifts be given to them** |

The pronoun of the second qualification must be recorded: *ātū**hunna*** — "give them". **The bridal gift is paid to the woman herself.** This is the repetition here of the ruling in the fourth verse.

### ٱلْعَنَت — the word

**The root ع-ن-ت: to fall into hardship, to be hard pressed; and the re-breaking of a broken bone.** The lexicographers record for the root the sense of "hardship, harm, a difficult position".

**The word occurs at `002-bakara.md` 2/220 as well** (*wa law shāʾa'llāhu la-aʿnatakum*), and there it is in the context of the orphan's property. **In both places the word concerns the qualification that a ruling should not press a person too hard.**

### وَأَن تَصْبِرُوا۟ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ — the closing

**The verse gives a permission, then says "that you be patient is better for you".**

**This structure recurs in the sūra, and I record it as my own reading:**

| Verse | Permission | What is set beside it |
|---|---|---|
| 4/25 | Permission to marry | *Wa an taṣbirū khayrun lakum* |
| 4/128 | Permission to make an arrangement | *Wa'l-ṣulḥu khayr* |
| 4/149 | Permission to speak out | *In tubdū khayran aw tukhfūhu aw taʿfū ʿan sūʾin fa-inna'llāha kāna ʿAfuwwan Qadīrā* |

**In all three places a ruling is laid down and what is better is shown beside it.** This is the same line recorded in the section on divorce in `002-bakara.md`: *"the rulings are laid down in detail, **but beside every ruling a door of latitude is left open**."* **I rely on that treatment.**

---

## 4/26-28 — يريد الله ليبين لكم ويهديكم سنن الذين من قبلكم ويتوب عليكم … ويريد الذين يتبعون الشهوات أن تميلوا ميلا عظيما · يريد الله أن يخفف عنكم وخلق الإنسان ضعيفا

*Yurīdu'llāhu li-yubayyina lakum wa yahdiyakum sunana'lladhīna min qablikum wa yatūba ʿalaykum, wa'llāhu ʿAlīmun Ḥakīm · Wa'llāhu yurīdu an yatūba ʿalaykum wa yurīdu'lladhīna yattabiʿūna'l-shahawāti an tamīlū maylan ʿaẓīmā · Yurīdu'llāhu an yukhaffifa ʿankum wa khuliqa'l-insānu ḍaʿīfā*

"God wishes to make things clear to you, to show you the ways of those before you, and to accept your repentance. God is Knowing, Wise. God wishes to accept your repentance; but those who follow their desires wish you to swerve utterly away. God wishes to lighten your burden. Man was created weak."

### Four acts of will in three verses

**In these three verses the verb يُرِيدُ occurs four times, three of them ascribed to God and one to the other party. This is a countable fact.**

| Order | Who | What is willed |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | God | *Li-yubayyina lakum* — **to make clear** |
| 2 | God | *An yatūba ʿalaykum* — **to accept repentance** |
